- **Name line.** The character's name in its own neon, then a small player marker: YOU or RIVAL online, P1 or P2 for a local player, CPU 7 for the computer at difficulty 7, TRAINER for a test scene's script, the stage (STAGE 3, BOSS) in the arcade ladder. The arcade score sits after it.
- **Move hints.** The line under the name cycles through that character's special moves every 3.6 seconds, input first in amber and then the name: `→→3 EXPRESS SLIDE`, `←2 SHOULDER CHECK`, `↓3, 3, 2 WRITE-OFF`, `1, 2, 3, 4 NEON TORNADO`. It reads the character's `moveList` (src/characters/), so a new character's moves show up with no HUD change. Arrows follow the move lists: → is forward, toward the opponent. Both bars show their own fighter's moves, so it teaches the matchup too. A keyboard screen adds the move list's short note; a touch screen (`touch-ui`) shows just the input and the name, bigger. Happy accidents appear only once this browser has found them (`discovered()` in `src/flow/techniques.js`, read only), as `3 › 4 SCISSOR KICK` in the accident green, and only for a character who can do them.
- **Overhead status track.** A compact readout over each fighter's head (the hero and the one he's on in horde), so you can read the fight without looking at the corners. Only what's active shows, top line first:
  - STUN (hitstun) with a red bar that drains to the middle as the stun runs out; GUARD (blockstun) with a cyan one; GUARD or LOW GUARD, dimmer, while the guard is up; FAINT with a violet bar (it drains faster with each head shake) and two stars chasing round the word; DOWN while knocked down, with a violet bar draining over the second before he stands up by himself, GET UP with a filling bar; a botch's coaching line (TOO FAST?!, BOTH ARMS?!: see Botches and happy accidents) in orange, a size bigger than the other tags, for 1.1 seconds and for as long as the stumble lasts, on top of whatever else is going on (with the stun's bar under it if he's hit mid-stumble); ARMOR while Security's shoulder check still has its armor; PARRY while Helpdesk's stance is live; OPEN during a whiffed strike's recovery (punish it).
  - Timed statuses (the sim's `status` and `selfStatus` hooks), a small tag each under the main line with a bar draining to the left: WRITTEN UP (pink, HR REP's Write-Up: half stamina regen) and WIRED (lime, BARISTA's Espresso Shot: faster startups). The tag is whatever the move's hook names.
  - SHOCKED (yellow, with the stun's bar) instead of STUN while a shock runs (frame data `shock`, ELECTRICIAN's Live Wire); STANCE while a stance is up (frame data `stance`, WELLNESS COACH's Tree Pose); ROOTED (yellow, a draining bar) while a root holds (frame data `root`, ELECTRICIAN's Ground Fault).
  - Flashes for half a second: COUNTER on the fighter who ate a counter hit, ARMOR when the armor absorbs one, PARRY when a stance or a timed tap catches one.
  - A thin stamina bar under it once any stamina is spent (amber, pink and flashing under 35, violet while fainted); it lingers a beat after refilling and then goes, so an idle fighter shows nothing.
  - Diamond pips for the air jumps left while a Window Washer is in the air.
  - It sits above the head, slides sideways off the other fighter, clamps on screen below the hint line, moves beside the head when a jump takes it to the top, and fades to a ghost when it has nowhere clear to go. The callouts step around it. It maps through `camera.mirror`, so the `?mirror=1` debug view still lines up.
- **Callouts and the hit counter** stack on the attacker's side under the hint line, else the other side, else the middle; banners (FIGHT, KO) take the first free slot. A botch isn't in this list: its line is drawn over the fighter who botched, in the overhead status track. A happy accident found for the first time gets TECHNIQUE FOUND under them.
- **On the bodies** (`src/fx/status.js`, drawn on the FX emissive layer, which the compositor hides wherever a fighter is, so these show only as rims and glints around them): yellow-white arcs crackling off the joints while shocked; a pulsing yellow ring round each foot while rooted; a red halo and red light in hitstun that fade as the stun drains (thin and faint after a light hit, fuller after a medium one or three hits into a combo, the full pulsing halo after a counter); nothing for a guard that's just held (the guard mark in the fighter's color reads it), and a brief thin white-cyan glint arc on the guard side of a blocked strike, sized by the strike; stars circling the head and a wobbling violet ring while fainted (three, fewer as the faint runs down, and each head shake knocks one off, flying out the way the head whipped); an amber halo while armored and a flare when it absorbs; a white-cyan shield ring in front of a parry stance; a hot white halo and a harder body flash on a counter hit; a cyan ring kicked out under the feet when an air jump is spent; breath puffs under 35 stamina, plus sweat flicks and steam when nearly empty or fainted; a gold glint over the head when a happy accident lands. Tier 1 (`?tier=1`) drops the sweat, steam and the halos' outer pass.
- **Hit effects scale with the hit's weight** (`hitWeight` in `src/fx/status.js`, used by `main.js`; 2026-11-09, the owner: "Jabs can spark. But I don't think it needs the big ring effect"). Light (jab, low jab, the light kicks, anything under 9 damage that doesn't knock down): the contact flash and about 11 short, slow sparks, no ring, almost no shake. Medium (cross, body blow, 9+ damage that doesn't knock down): about 17 sparks, a small light, still no ring. Heavy (enders, launchers, knockdowns, splats, bounces, KOs): the full burst with the amber shockwave ring (`ring` on `fx.hit`, out to a radius of about 210 px on a KO), the smoke disc and the full shake and zoom punch, as before. A counter reads one step heavier, and its cyan and magenta double ring (`fx.counterFlash`, `scale`) shows on every weight because it's a signal, sized to the hit: about half size on a jab counter. Blocked hits get their own small set (next bullet). Shake on the release of the freeze is scaled by the same weight: peak camera offset on combo-test's jabs went from 15 px to 4, the cross from 17 to 10. Fighters never glow: all of this is on the FX layer around them.
- **Block effects scale the same way** (`blockWeight` in `src/fx/status.js`, used by `main.js`'s `BLOCK_FX` and `fx.guardSpark`; 2026-11-09, the owner: a block showed "a big blue shield effect, too much for something this common"). The big shield was the dashed cyan arc that status.js drew in front of every raised guard, brighter for all of blockstun, with a cyan light; it's gone. Holding guard with nothing coming now draws nothing on the FX layer (the guard mark in the fighter's color on the body still shows it). A blocked strike gets a small chevron glyph at the contact, a tiny cool-white core with a few short sparks kicked back at the attacker, a thin white-cyan glint arc on the guard side, and a faint light, all gone in 0.07 to 0.15 s. Light (a jab, a light kick: under 9 damage, no knockdown): the chevron at 55% size with two spark lines, 4 sparks, a 14 px glint, a little shake (trauma 0.015). Medium (a cross, a body blow, a sweep into down-back: 9+ damage or a knockdown): 75%, 6 sparks, 19 px, trauma 0.03. Heavy (an ender, a launcher, a splat, 14+ damage): the full chevron as before, 10 sparks, a 26 px glint, trauma 0.06, the old block shake. There's no ring and no zoom punch on any block (a block never releases a hit freeze), and the block sound was already scaled by the strike's damage (`sfx.block`). A parry keeps its PARRY flash, white ring and halo and the full chevron, a broken throw keeps the full chevron, and HELPDESK's stance ring is untouched. There's no guard crush in the game yet, so a future one should get its own signal instead of reusing these. `guard-test` phases 7 to 9 (a blocked jab, a sweep into down-back, 1,1,2,4 into a guard) show the three weights.
- The HUD and the status effects only read the sim (and its events, forwarded by `main.js` through `hud.event` and `statusFX.event`); they never write to it, so the sim stays deterministic.

### Camera

`updateCamera()` in `src/main.js` frames the fight the way Tekken does: two fighters in each other's face fill the frame's height. Everything is computed from the bodies, `sim.bounds` and the floors' data, never from a fixed stage width, so a wider stage needs no camera change.

- **Close range.** The view is the taller fighter's standing height (floor to the top of the head in the idle stance, from `rig._solve(STANCE)`, cached per body) plus 4 head heights of air over the head and 1 under the feet, where a head is `2 × rig.radius`. TEMP is 127 px tall with a 30 px head, which makes the view 277 world px tall and the zoom 2.60 on the 720 px frame; SECURITY (135 px, head 31) gives 2.46 and INTERN (121 px, head 29) 2.69. The feet sit 1 head over the bottom edge.
- **Apart.** The zoom eases out so both roots plus a leg's reach each side (0.95 of standing height, about 120 px) fit across the frame, down to the old wide framing (zoom 1) at full-screen distance. Between the two the vertical anchor blends from feet-at-the-bottom to the old center (the floor line less 120), so the wide shot is what it always was.
- **Nothing crops.** Every live body's drawn joints stay inside the frame: 28 px from the sides, 10 px from the bottom, and under the HUD strip at the top (`HUD_TOP` from `src/combat/hud.js`, the bars, names and move hints, plus 12 px). A jump or a launch pans up first and eases the zoom out only when the pan isn't enough. Two halves do this. `updateCamera()` steers its eased target from a prediction of where each body will be drawn, with a 48 px soft margin so it starts moving early. Then `fitCameraToDrawn()` runs in `render()` after `frameDraw()` has placed the frame's real drawing (the turnaround, snap fade and air catch-up) and clamps the camera to it. The prediction never forces anything, so a pose that jumps ahead of its drawing (the vault's drop, an air recovery) can't pop the frame.
- **Easing.** The zoom pushes in slowly (rate 3) and pulls out quickly (rate 9). A flying body is followed hard, as before. A new round cuts straight to the spawns instead of swooping back from the last round's floor.
- **Two floors.** A floor break or the follow-down frames both floors the same way, with the zoom allowed down to 0.85 as before. Horde frames the hero, the goon he's on and any other goon within 420 px of him.
- **Effects.** The zoom punch on a heavy hit and the KO slow-mo lean-in (12% for a finisher, 6.5% otherwise) are shares of the framing, so they read the same at any zoom, and neither pushes past the fit. Shake is scaled by `1.3 / zoom` so it's the same size on screen as at the old framing. Hitstop still freezes the camera. The view may still run 120 px past either wall. The far skyline is drawn at identity and placed by parallax, so `farDrawnW` caching is unaffected, and nothing in the arena or the fighters is a cached bitmap: at 2.6 everything is still vector-drawn at the layer's resolution.
- **The safe width** (`camera.safeW` and `camera.padTop` in `src/camera.js`, read by `camSafe()` in `src/main.js`). The camera frames inside a span of the frame, normally all 1280 px of it. A phone held upright shows only the middle of it, 720 px when the view is square and up to all 1280 on a short screen (Controls, Held upright: the view), so `main.js` sets `safeW` to the crop from the layout and the same camera does the rest: both fighters, their reach and the 28 px margin stay inside those 720 px, so it zooms out sooner as they separate (close range sits at about 2.0 to 2.5 where the full frame holds 2.6); the wide end is 0.54 in place of 1, the same world width in fewer px; walls, jumps, launches, splats, throws, floor breaks, the stairs and horde floors go through the same fit. With no bars in the frame the top margin is 44 px (`SQUARE_TOP`), room for an overhead status line. The full frame's zoom stops at 0.85, as it always has, so on the wide building two fighters more than about 1440 px apart walk out of the sides; the narrow span goes on down to whatever holds the whole building (about 0.28), so at its far walls both are still on screen, 17 px tall. The camera never feeds the sim, so rollback and online play don't see any of this.
  - **Checked** (2026-11-11): `scripts/shots/square-fit.mjs` steps 16 scenes for 1,500 frames each at 390x844 (three CPU fights, ko, juggle, wall, throw, jump-in, air recovery, floor break, stairs, both hordes, the Window Washer, getup, the perfect finisher): every drawn joint of the hero and the one he's on stayed inside the square on every frame. `scripts/shots/camtrace.mjs` steps nine scenes for 900 frames in the build before this and the build after, at 1280x720, 844x390 (touch) and 1024x768 (touch): the camera's x, y and zoom are the same on every frame. `npm run netsim:proof`: 0 of 17,672 restored steps differed. Again (2026-11-11, the variable crop) at 390x844, 412x915, 375x667, 395x700 and 412x640 (crops 720, 720, 865, 834 and 979): all 80 runs stayed inside the view, and camtrace was identical to the build before on all 27 runs; `npm run netsim:proof`: 0 of 20,755 restored steps differed.
- **Phones.** Held upright the camera frames inside the view's crop (above). On a phone held sideways the gutters are narrow and the stick and the button cluster sit over the stage, so `touchInsets()` measures how far they reach into it (twice a second, from the layout) and the camera keeps the bodies between them. Up close nothing changes; apart, the zoom is lower so neither fighter stands under the buttons.

**Measured** (2026-11-09, Playwright Chromium at 1280x720, every frame outside hitstop): no drawn head or foot left the frame or went under the HUD strip in close range, mid range, full-screen apart, jump-in-test, juggle-test, Sky Spike, air-recovery-test, all three floor-break-test phases, wall-test, the Window Washer cross-up, horde-ai (3,000 to 4,000 frames), horde-floors-ai, ko, getup-test, stomp-test, ai-fight, ai-fight-2, ai-fight-3 and three attract bouts. Frame to frame (ai-fight, 3,000 frames), the zoom moves 0.02% at the median and 2.3% at the 99th percentile, and the pan 0.2 px at the median and 17.6 px at the 99th percentile. The only bigger jumps are the round-start cuts.

### Menus

One scheme on every screen, kept in `src/flow/controls.js` (the same table is at the top of that file; change both together). The pad labels follow the pad: Xbox letters by default, PlayStation shapes when the browser's `gamepad.id` says it's a PlayStation pad.

| Action | Keyboard | Gamepad (Xbox / PlayStation) | Touch |
|---|---|---|---|
| Move the focus | arrows, WASD (not on the two typing screens) | D-pad, left stick | - |
| Confirm | Enter, Space | A / ✕ | tap the button |
| Back | Esc, Backspace (Backspace deletes on the typing screens) | B / ○, Select | the BACK button |
| Start | - | Start / Options: on the title it starts, in a fight it pauses (online: the leave confirm), in the pause menu it resumes, anywhere else it's confirm | - |
| Delete (initials, room code, a name, a crew, an email, a code) | Backspace | X / □ (the console keyboard convention) | DEL |
| Move list (pause menu, character select, NEW CHARACTERS) | F1 | Y / △ | the MOVE LIST button |
| SETTINGS panel | the menu button (click), or SETTINGS in the pause menu; Esc closes | SETTINGS in the pause menu; B closes | the menu button, or SETTINGS in the pause menu; X closes |
| Nothing | - | X anywhere else, Y where there's no move list, bumpers, triggers, R3 | - |

- A direction moves the focus on the press. Held on a pad it repeats after 250 ms and then every 80 ms (`REPEAT_FIRST` and `REPEAT_EVERY` in `menuInput.js`; 250 ms is longer than a tap, so one tap is one step); a held key repeats at the OS's own rate.
- An item taking the focus lights at once, with no fade in; the one losing it fades out (80 to 90 ms).
- B never confirms and A never backs out. Screens with nothing behind them (the title, a stage card, stage clear, CONTINUE?, game over, initials, the ending) ignore back instead of guessing.
- Every screen opens with the focus on its main action, the focus never lands on a dim item (JOIN before the code is whole, COPY LINK before the room opens), and the D-pad reaches every button (the walkthrough checks this by searching every direction from every item).
- **The legend** along the bottom of each screen lists that screen's actions in the glyphs of the device in use: keycaps (ENTER SELECT · ESC BACK) after a key or a mouse click, round face buttons in the standard colors (A green, B red, X blue, Y yellow; PlayStation ✕ ○ □ △) and a START or OPTIONS pill after a pad press, and nothing on touch, where the buttons are tapped. It switches the moment the player picks up something else (`<html>` gets `dev-key`, `dev-pad` or `dev-touch`, plus `pad-ps`). In a fight there's no legend, only the small corner hint (ESC PAUSE, or START PAUSE on a pad), so nothing covers the fighters.

### Move list

A full command list for one fighter, like Tekken's (`src/flow/movelist.js`). It opens from the pause menu's MOVE LIST (the fighter you're playing) and from the character select's MOVE LIST (the fighter under the cursor), and F1 or pad Y opens it on either screen. It's an overlay, not a flow state, so the screen under it keeps its state (the game stays paused, the cursor stays put) and any screen change closes it. It covers the whole viewport rather than the 16:9 stage, so a phone held upright gets the full screen: the preview on top, the list under it.

- **Sections**, with a tab each: UNIQUE MOVES (SIGNATURE MOVES for TEMP, who has none), STRINGS, BASIC ATTACKS, SYSTEM and HAPPY ACCIDENTS. Unique moves are the character file's `moveList`, then any own move it doesn't name. Strings are the character's whole table (`c.strings`: the shared ones it keeps and its own, its own first with a UNIQUE chip), each with its hits, about how much damage it does from the first hit with combo decay, and chips for its payoff (knockdown, launch, wall splat, overhead, low...); dropped shared strings are named under them. Basic attacks are the shared button table with each move's height and payoff, struck through where one of the character's commands takes the input. System is the moves with no button of their own (walk, guard, low guard, crouch, parry, jump, run, throw break, get up, roll, air recovery) plus a row for a double jump or a wall jump. Happy accidents are the eight techniques (only those this character can do): found ones by name and buttons, the rest ??? with their hint.
- **All of it is read from the game's data** by `moveListOf()` in `src/combat/moveinfo.js`, which also holds the shared button table, the system rows and the notation, and which `site/data.mjs` imports for the fighter pages, so the two can't drift. A new character shows up with no change here.
- **The strings note.** The strings section opens with the rule people miss: *strings need every hit to connect (hit or blocked); if one whiffs, the next press is a mash and you botch*. Under it, a steady beat about 0.1 to 0.5 s between presses. Measured in the dummy scene with real key presses, TEMP's Neon Tornado landed all four hits with 60, 100, 150, 200, 300, 400, 500 and 600 ms between presses (three tries each) and dropped to 2 or 3 at 750 ms; the same presses from out of range whiffed the jab and botched.
- **Inputs** are written the way the HUD's move hints write them (→ toward, ← away, ↓, the button numbers), and after a pad press the numbers turn into the pad's own face buttons (1 X, 2 Y, 3 A, 4 B, or the PlayStation shapes), from `faceGlyph()` in `src/flow/controls.js`.
- **The preview.** Highlighting a row plays that move on the character's rig in the side pane, a string's presses chained at each recover key the way the fighter pages' move cards do (`site/rigs.js`), and replays it every second or so. System rows and accidents not yet found stand idle.
- **Controls.** Up and down move the highlight, left and right jump a section, confirm replays the preview, and back, Start, F1 or Y close it. On touch and with the mouse, tap a row, tap a tab, scroll the list, and CLOSE closes. The 30 second idle return doesn't run while it's open.
- **Scrolling**, by input. The list (`.ml-list`) is the only thing that scrolls up and down; the page under it never moves.
  - **Touch:** drag a finger on the list. It's the browser's own scrolling, so it has the phone's momentum and stops at the list's ends (`overscroll-behavior: contain`) instead of pulling the page. A drag is never a tap: the highlight only moves when a row is tapped. Held upright the five tabs are wider than the screen, and they scroll sideways the same way. This didn't work until 2026-11-11: `src/touch.js` cancels every `touchmove` on the page so nothing pans in a fight, and that cancelled the list's scrolling too. The list and the tabs are now marked `data-scroll` (`y` and `x`), which the guard leaves alone (see Touch, below). A drag that starts on the preview or the header scrolls nothing.
  - **Mouse wheel and trackpad:** scroll anywhere over the overlay. Over the list it's the browser's; over the preview, the header or the tabs the overlay passes the wheel on to the list. The scrollbar drags as usual (thin, violet, at the list's right edge; macOS shows it only while scrolling unless the system is set to always show scrollbars).
  - **Keyboard and pad:** there's no scroll key. Up and down move the highlight and the list follows it (`select()` calls `scrollIntoView` with `block: 'nearest'`), so every row is whole when it's highlighted, the last one included. Left and right jump to the section's first row and put its heading at the top.
  - **The lit tab** is the highlight's section until the list is scrolled by hand; then it follows the section on screen (the one a third of the way down the list, or the last at the very bottom), and left and right jump from there. The highlight and the preview stay where they were until a key or a tap.
  - `npm run test:movelist` (`scripts/movelist/check.mjs`) drives all of it in headless Chromium with real touch gestures at 844x390 and 390x844 and the wheel and keys at 1280x720, on the fighter with the longest list with every happy accident found, from the character select and the pause menu, and with `--online` on a guest's online pick screen. It can't drag the scrollbar: headless Chromium draws none.
- **Online.** It isn't offered mid-match: online has no pause, and reading a list while your fighter stands there would hand the rival free hits. The pick screens have it (your first pick, and NEW CHARACTERS between matches), and it only covers your own screen there: the rival's game and any countdown carry on, and a match starting closes it. F1's quick strip still works in an online fight.

### Strings and enders

Press the buttons in rhythm; each press goes in during the previous hit's cancel window, so a string that starts on a hit lands as a true combo.

| Input | Name | Payoff |
|---|---|---|
| 1,1,2 | One-Two | |
| 1,1,1 | Triple Jab | |
| 1,2,4 | One-Two Kick | knockdown |
| 2,3 | Cross Snap | |
| 1,1,2,4 | Flash Roundhouse | knockdown |
| 1,2,3 | Uptown | mid finish |
| 1,2,3,4 | Neon Tornado | launches into a juggle |
| 2,1,4 | Spin Heel | wall splat |
| 3,3,4 | Crescent | knockdown |
| d+1,d+1,d+3 | Low Rush | three lows from a crouch |
| d+3,d+3,4 | Ground Rush | low, low, mid knockdown |
| d+1, release down, 3 | Rise and Shine | the crouch jab into the rising kick |
| d+2, then 1,2 in the air, then 4 | Sky Spike | ground bounce, relaunch for one more hit |
| f,f+2 (from a dash or a run) | Comet Cross | wall splat near a wall |
| f,f+1 / f,f+3 / f,f+4 | Bullet Jab / Skid Kick / Running Boot | the other dash attacks: a fast high, a low, a slow mid that knocks down |
| b+4 near a wall | Wall Spin | wall bounce into a juggle |
| throw near a wall | Wall Slam | wall splat |

Trick shots: a **wall splat** sticks the body to the wall for 0.4s, open for one more hit. A **wall bounce** and a **ground bounce** relaunch it. A **counter hit** (hitting someone during their startup) does 35% more damage and stun. If both strikes land on the same frame it's a **trade**: both react. If the two striking limbs meet instead, it's a **clash** (below): nobody is hit. Juggles get heavier with each airborne hit and end after four. A KO by an ender gets a slow-motion beat.

**The clash.** Two fighters throw at the same moment, fist meets fist or shin meets shin, and neither one is hit: a blue spark flies where the limbs met, CLASH comes up, both freeze a beat with the limbs still touching, bounce back a step and are free on the same frame. It's a dead-even read, so nobody wins it and nobody gets plus frames. The rules (`clashTest()` in `src/combat/sim.js`, the numbers in `CLASH` in `src/combat/frameData.js`):

- **The limbs have to meet.** Each strike's striking segment as it's drawn (the forearm and fist of a punch, the shin and foot of a kick, whatever the move's `limb` / `joint` says, `strikeSegment()` in `src/combat/hitboxes.js`) touches the other's, within the two drawn limbs plus 2 px (`slack`), on a step where both strikes are live (active, or inside the early window a strike can already land in). Limb against limb is tested before limb against body, so limbs that meet a step before a fist would have reached a head still clash.
- **Hitting a limb never does damage.** While a strike of the kind that can clash is on its way (startup or active, not yet connected), its striking segment isn't body to another such strike: a fist that only reaches the other fist has to carry on to the head or the chest to hit. The rest of the arm or leg, the other limbs and the body are hit as before, and so is a limb left out in recovery (a whiff is still punished at the wrist).
- **Different heights still trade.** A jab at the head and a snap kick at the body pass each other and both land: a TRADE, as before. Two sweeps land on each other's standing leg before the shins meet, so they trade too. A low kick ducks under a high punch and simply wins.
- **Heavy against light breaks through.** A strike whose damage in the frame data (or its `weight`, when a move sets one) is `breakBy` 8 or more above the other's isn't stopped: the lighter limb is batted aside (that strike ends and he reels 10 frames, open; it's not a counter hit), and the heavy one carries on and lands the ordinary way. So jab (6) against jab, cross (10), snap kick (7) or lunge cross (11) clashes, cross against roundhouse (16) clashes, roundhouse against roundhouse clashes, and a roundhouse, a spin kick (15) or any ender (14 to 18) breaks through a jab; FLASH ROUNDHOUSE (18) breaks through a cross.
- **Never a clash:** a throw or a command grab, a counter stance (a parry that catches a strike is still a parry) or a throw counter, a stance, a `noHit` move, an armored move (SECURITY's Shoulder Check and CHEF's Cleaver keep their armor: they absorb, as ever), an invulnerable one, a wake-up strike, the stomp, a botch's flail, a graze, and any strike in the air (an anti-air is a read: the uppercut hits the body, it doesn't stop at the foot). The universal parry isn't a strike, so it's untouched.
- **What it costs.** No damage, no hitstun, no combo, no counter hit and no refund: both paid full price for the swing, so a masher who clashes is still gassing out, and a clash on the press that emptied the bar faints him as a block would. Both freeze 4 frames (`hitstop`), then recoil for 14 (`stun`) on the throw break's push-off clip, sliding back about 35 px each, and both can act on the same step. On the contact frame, tips that ran past each other are backed out to meet tip to tip (each body gives up to `seat` 24 px). Anything buffered is dropped.
- **Mashing loses, still.** A botched press isn't a strike (it's a stumble, or the flail), so it can't clash. The window is the same dead-even one a trade had: both strikes have to go live within a step or two of each other, since a strike that's live first reaches the body first and is a counter hit. Nothing held or repeated makes a clash more likely, and the CPU (`src/combat/ai.js`) has no idea the clash exists: it clashes by accident, like a person.
- **Online.** It's sim state: `u.clashUntil` (the bounce back) rides the snapshot and the state hash with `tradeUntil` / `tradeLimb`, and `NET_VERSION` is 23.
- **What you see and hear.** `onEvent({ type: 'clash', x, y, a, b, units })` at the contact point (`broke: true` for a break-through) and `note({ type: 'clash', ... })` in the log. `src/main.js` draws the block's blue chevron a touch bigger and both ways with its cool light, a small shake and camera kick; `sfx.clash` is the block's clack made sharp, a double crack and a short ring, lower for shins. The fighters aren't tinted. The limbs stay out of the solid-body push until the freeze lets go (the trade's `tradeLimb` / `tradeUntil`), so the fists hold at the contact.

`?scene=clash-test` plays each case (see the scene list).

**Measurements (the clash, 2026-11-10).** Main, then this build, same seeds.
- **Scripted scenes** (`node gauntlet/measure.mjs scenes`, and again with `--root` on a checkout of main; a page of its own per scene, a digest of both bodies' state and every joint on every frame): 258 of the 261 phases that exist on both builds are identical frame for frame. The three that aren't: trade-test's first phase (two jabs on one step were the trade; their fists meet, so the phase is now a jab against a snap kick, which still trades), and the CPU fights ai-fight-2 and ko, where a cross against a cross on the same step was a trade and is now a clash, after which the fight goes its own way.
- **Mashers** (`node gauntlet/measure.mjs mash --matches 30`; rounds the masher won, his damage, clashes and break-throughs, trades). Random mashing at CPU 0.3: 0 / 0 rounds, 1651 / 1578 damage, 1 clash, 2 / 0 trades. At 0.7: 0 / 0 rounds, 1547 / 1338, 5 clashes and 2 break-throughs, 11 / 2 trades. Mashing 1 at 0.3: 2 / 1 rounds, 3813 / 3765, 2 clashes, 4 / 0 trades. At 0.7: 0 / 0 rounds, 2765 / 2621, 3 clashes and 1 break-through, 6 / 4 trades. Mashers win no more and deal a little less: a clash takes away the trade that used to pay a masher 6 for 6, and about one clash in ten matches is nothing to lean on.
- **CPU vs CPU at 0.7** (`node gauntlet/measure.mjs cpu --matches 12 --pairs ...`, sides swapped every match; rounds won by the first name, then clashes and trades over the 12 matches). TEMP v TEMP 15-18 / 17-14, 6 clashes, 8 / 3 trades. TEMP v COURIER 21-8 / 17-12, 4 clashes, 7 / 3. SECURITY v HELPDESK 11-17 / 9-20, 6 clashes, 18 / 6. THE MANAGER v INTERN 12-13 / 12-18, none, 2 / 0. THE CEO v JANITOR 10-18 / 11-18, 6 clashes, 9 / 5. CHEF v BARISTA 15-18 / 17-10, 3 clashes, 4 / 1. In all 25 clashes in 72 matches, about one every three matches, no break-throughs, and trades down from 48 to 18; the first-named side won 84 of 176 rounds before and 83 of 175 after. A pairing's split moves because a match plays out differently from its first clash on, and 12 matches is a small sample.
- **Rollback.** `npm run netsim:proof`: 1,992 rollbacks, 0 of 17,086 restored steps differed, and 24 resyncs with 0 of 5,760 differing. A second check saved and restored through five scripted clashes and a break-through: 630 rollbacks (198 of them saved inside a clash's freeze or bounce), 0 of 4,104 restored steps differed.

### Botches and happy accidents

Mashing doesn't just wait in the buffer any more. A strike pressed in the middle of another one, before that one is allowed to finish, fights it for the same body, and the fighter messes up.

**The rule.** Everything that legally carries on from a strike behaves exactly as before: a string's next press, a listed cancel (a move the CPU names), a throw pressed a frame after a jab or kick (1, then 3 a beat late), any press after a launch, knockdown or stomp connected (their recovery is cut short for the follow-up), any press while your juggle is in the air or on the wall, and anything in the last 8 steps (about 4 frames) of the recovery, which still buffers into the next move. Any other strike pressed during the startup, the active frames or the rest of the recovery is **mistimed** and botches. Throws, specials and character moves follow the same rule; holding a direction, guarding, jumping and moving never botch. In the air a second air attack pressed while the first is still swinging tangles you up (the jab on the way down comes only once the first has recovered) (after it connects, or just before you land, it buffers for the landing like before). A tangled fighter loses his presses until he's back.

**What happens comes from the forces involved.** The sim measures the strike that was out (which limb, which way it turns the body, startup, active or recovery, how fast the hips and the striking limb were moving, the lean, a glide or a lunge under it) against what the new press wants (its limb, its height, forward or not), and that picks one of five families:

| Family | What the player is told | Typical cause | What it looks like |
|---|---|---|---|
| Overbalance | TOO FAST?! · WHOA, EASY! · SLOW DOWN! | a forward strike on top of a lunge, a glide or a deep lean | lurches forward and catches himself with two steps, open the whole time |
| Spin-out | WRONG WAY?! · DIZZY?! · WHICH WAY?! | the body still turning one way (a spin kick, a rear-side strike) while the new strike turns it the other | the turn carries him round until his back is to you, a stooped beat, then round again; a hit while his back is turned is a counter |
| Trip | TWO LEGS?! · FEET! · ONE KICK! | a kick on top of a kick (worst with the same leg still up), a low on a kick | the feet cross and he goes down: a self-knockdown on the point simulation, then the usual getup |
| Flail | BOTH ARMS?! · STOP MASHING! · ONE PUNCH! | both arms at once | a weak, ugly double swing (it can touch, 2 to 3 damage, but never extends a combo) and he hangs there blowing |
| Whiff | TAKE YOUR TIME! · WAIT FOR IT! · NOT YET! | the first strike mostly done | the new limb shoots out half-formed and hangs there, a long recovery |
| Air tangle | ONE AT A TIME! · EASY UP THERE! · MID-AIR?! | a second air attack while the first is still swinging (not one of the five: it's its own rule) | the body tumbles out of the air and lands in a heap |

**What the player is told** (2026-11-10, the owner: "over balance" sounds like "extra medium"; nobody could tell what they did wrong or that it was their own fault). A botch doesn't show its family's name. It shows a short coaching line in plain words, one of the three in the table (`BOTCH_LINES` in `src/combat/botch.js`; about 14 characters at most, no jargon, never the family's name), over the head of the fighter who botched, not in the corner callout list. It's the top line of his overhead status track (`botchOf` in `src/combat/hud.js`), so it follows him, steps off the other fighter, stays on screen and lines up in a mirrored view. It pops in, stays 1.1 seconds and for as long as the stumble lasts, and it's plain text: solid orange (`KIND_COLOR.botch`, the mistake color; hits are red, counters amber, enders the player's own color) in a dark outline, no glow. COUNTER, enders, HAPPY ACCIDENT and the rest are where they were. The old BOTCH tag and the TANGLED tag over a downed fighter are gone (he reads DOWN once the line is done).

The line is picked at draw time by `botchLine(kind, id, botchAt)`: a hash of the unit's id and the step it botched on, both already in the sim's state. It draws nothing from `sim.rng` and stores nothing, so both peers and a rollback show the same line. The sim is untouched: it still pushes the same callout record and log entry it always did (`{ text: BOTCH_CALLOUT[kind], kind: 'botch' }`, the old family label, which is now only a label for `gauntlet/measure.mjs`, the logs and the test scenes, and is never drawn), so saved state is bit-identical to the build before and `NET_VERSION` didn't move. The HUD shows the line while that record lives, so a rollback that undoes the botch takes the line with it.

Every botch costs stamina (8 + 0.6 of the new move's price, more for two kicks, an ender under it or a harder botch, most for a flail; 20 to 40 in practice), and a botch with under 30 left empties the bar: he faints standing (never a trip, since a knockdown would wake him).

None of it is a canned animation. Each family is built on the spot from those numbers and the pose the body is in (`play('botch', { botch })` in `src/fighter/index.js`): play() blends into it from the current pose carried forward by its momentum, the trip and the tangle hand the body to the point simulation (`launch`), and the flail is a real, weak strike. Positions never teleport: a stumble's travel stops short of the other body and the wall, the root takes the step when the clip ends (or when a hit cuts it short), bodies stay solid, and sides follow the usual rules.

**Unpredictable, but the same every time.** The family comes from the forces, quantized to small integers (hip speed in 40 px/s steps, limb speed in 100 px/s steps, the lean in 5 degree steps, the phase), plus one draw from `sim.rng` for variety. That makes a botch feel random but exactly reproducible from the same state, which is what lets a player find the techniques, and it keeps rollback and every browser engine bit-identical. The new per-fighter state is plain unit fields (`botchKind`, `botchSev`, `botchShift`, `botchFwd`, `botchAt`, `activeAt`, `recoverAt`), which the snapshot copies; the generated clips ride the rig's `getState()` like any move. `NET_VERSION` is 7.

**Happy accidents.** A few presses that would botch, landing in a narrow window of a particular strike, come out as a hidden technique instead, with a HAPPY ACCIDENT callout: SCISSOR KICK, SUPERMAN PUNCH, PIVOT BACKFIST, FLASH KICK, HELICOPTER, FLYING TACKLE, CROSS KNEE and SPRING UPPERCUT. Everyone has all eight. They're strong (14 to 16 damage, launches, a wall splat, an unblockable tackle) and risky (long recoveries, little blockstun). They aren't in the move list until you find one; then the F1 move list shows it, the pause menu lists all eight as TECHNIQUES FOUND 3/8 (the rest as ???, each with a hint), and the character select shows the count. What you've found is kept in localStorage as `stick-fight-techniques`. A window's step count runs on the strike's own clock, so a hit's freeze holds it.

*Developer note:* the exact windows (which strike, which press, which steps) live in `ACCIDENTS` in `src/combat/botch.js`, and `?scene=accident-test` shows each one landing and the same press a step early and a step late botching instead. They're kept out of this section on purpose.

*Developer note:* the whiff, overbalance and spin-out mix the two moves per limb instead of playing a canned clip: the body carries on with the interrupted move (or the stumble) while the limb the new press wanted plays the new move's own keys part of the way, its elbow or knee trailing, and drags the torso a little after it (`src/fighter/limbBlend.js`). Trip, flail and the air tangle still use their own clips.

**The CPU.** It mostly holds a press that would botch until it's clean. The lower its difficulty, the more often it lets one go and mashes a button mid-strike on its own (about a third of strikes at INTERN's 0.2, a few percent at 0.7, never at 1.0 or for the showcase), so the bottom of the ladder shows the system off. Every CPU punishes a botch it sees, the better ones more often. The showcase (the attract demo and THE MANAGER) never botches but now and then presses into a technique's window for flavor.

**The switch.** On by default. BOTCHES: ON/OFF in the pause menu (from the next fight; kept in localStorage as `stick-fight-botch`), or `?botch=0` on the URL (`?botch=1` forces it on). Online, the host's setting rides the start message, so both sims play the same rules. With botches off the game is bit-for-bit the old one.

**Measurements.** Mashers against a TEMP CPU, three matches each, before and after: random mashing dealt 404 and 333 damage (CPU 0.3 and 0.7) before, 108 and 96 after, best combo 5 and 7 before, 1 and 3 after, and spent 39% and 50% of the fight open (botching, fainted or on the floor) before, 64% and 62% after. Mashing 1 alone dealt 444 and 406 before, 347 and 272 after (average combo 2.8 to 2.1 and 2.7 to 1.6). Every scripted scene (combo-test, string-test, scripted-combo, string-ender-test, jump-in-test, anti-air-test, wall-test, juggle-test, floor-break-test, guard-test, mixup-test, trade-test, throw-test and the eight character tests) gives the same max combos, callouts and damage as before; with botches off all 96 phases have the same per-step state hash as the build before botches. stamina-test, which is literally spam on a guard, now botches. Three test scripts pressed every few steps regardless of their own strike (floor-break-test's chase, helpdesk-test's and janitor-test's punishers); those presses were already thrown away by the buffer, and they now skip a press that would botch. CPU vs CPU at 0.7, 40 matches a pairing with sides swapped, rounds won before and after: TEMP v TEMP 46-51 / 54-47, TEMP v COURIER 60-34 / 55-45, SECURITY v HELPDESK 52-46 / 53-42, MANAGER v INTERN 56-44 / 61-39, JANITOR v WINDOW WASHER 59-43 / 60-41, ACCOUNTANT v TEMP 68-33 / 59-47 (inside the 10-point swing of the sample), with 1.5 to 3 botches a match. The attract demo shows no botches and a happy accident every few minutes. Save/restore: six recorded CPU bouts with botches and accidents (9,000 steps each, inputs, the rng state after each CPU call and `grabId`, `rollId`, `aaJump` recorded) replayed identically, then took 1,037 rollbacks of 1 to 16 steps (save, run, restore, run, restore, run), with save points inside botches and accidents: all 26,397 restored steps matched the reference's deep hash of `saveState()`; clearing `botchKind` or nudging `botchShift` on restore made it differ on the first step. The same bouts plus botch-test and accident-test hash identically on every step in Chromium, Firefox and WebKit.

**Measurements (stamina refunds, 2026-11-09).** `node gauntlet/measure.mjs mash` (TEMP mashers against a TEMP CPU, 60 matches per row here; random mashing is a random button every 10 to 18 steps with the stick re-rolled every 40, mashing 1 walks in and presses 1 every 12 steps). Before the refunds, then after (rounds the masher won, damage, best combo, share of the fight open: botching, fainted or on the floor; faints):

| masher | CPU | rounds | damage | best | open | faints |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| random | 0.3 | 0 / 0 | 3606 / 3209 | 4 / 5 | 62% / 55% | 401 / 227 |
| random | 0.7 | 0 / 0 | 2711 / 2998 | 5 / 8 | 60% / 60% | 252 / 160 |
| 1 | 0.3 | 5 / 3 (of about 123) | 7272 / 7564 | 5 / 5 | 54% / 36% | 525 / 252 |
| 1 | 0.7 | 0 / 0 | 5353 / 5014 | 4 / 4 | 48% / 45% | 316 / 163 |

Mashers still lose: the old build already dropped about one round in 25 to a 1-masher against the 0.3 CPU (3-match samples never show it), and the new one drops as many. Damage and best combo stay within about 10%. The masher faints about half as often (the jab that lands keeps him going), so against the 0.3 CPU he spends less of the fight open. Two things keep that in check, and both came out of these runs: without the half-price floor on the save, a fighter on 1 jabs forever on hits that leave him on 1, and the repeat decay keeps a jab string from paying for itself. A cornered dummy under clean jab strings as fast as they come out (20 s): combos still top out at 3 to 4 hits (combo decay), and the jabber still faints, 3 times against the old 6. CPU vs CPU at 0.7 (`measure.mjs cpu`, 40 matches a pairing, sides swapped), rounds before / after: TEMP v TEMP 50-51 / 50-50, TEMP v COURIER 57-45 / 55-40, SECURITY v HELPDESK 50-47 / 58-42, MANAGER v INTERN 56-46 / 56-35, JANITOR v WINDOW WASHER 61-39 / 61-38, ACCOUNTANT v TEMP 32-66 / 47-54 (the sample swings by 10 points or so; a 24-match run of SECURITY v HELPDESK on the old build came out 16-43). CPU faints fall to about a third (18-17 to 9-7 in TEMP v TEMP). Every timeline scene, phase by phase (`measure.mjs scene NAME`), against the build before: 228 of 231 phases give the same combo, damage, hits and blocks; the other three are NEON TORNADO and SKY SPIKE in string-ender-test and launch-then-mash-1 in juggle-test, which used to faint after the combo and now don't.

**Measurements (shaking off a faint, 2026-11-09).** `node gauntlet/measure.mjs mash --matches 30`, main then this branch (rounds the masher won, damage, best combo, open share, faints). Random mashing at CPU 0.3: 0 / 0 rounds, 1578 / 1739 damage, best 3 / 4, open 56.2% / 56.7%, 111 / 120 faints. Random at 0.7: 0 / 0, 1743 / 1685, 6 / 4, 60.5% / 61.5%, 89 / 93. Mashing 1 at 0.3: 3 / 1 rounds, 3953 / 3545, 5 / 4, 39.8% / 36.6%, 148 / 126. Mashing 1 at 0.7: 0 / 0, 2602 / 2765, 3 / 5, 45.7% / 44.6%, 80 / 81. Every row moves within the noise of 30 matches, with nothing better across the board, which fits: the 1-masher walks in holding forward, a held direction is one press, and the random masher re-rolls its stick only every 40 steps. Save/restore across faints (`measure.mjs faintsave`): 12 faints with shakes and jabs, a save every 9 steps through each, 40 steps run, loaded and run again: all 1,440 restored steps hash the same, and all 36 loads with `faintCut` or `shakeDir` nudged differ.

### Fundamentals

Four mechanics every character shares, built so the game is easy to learn and hard to master: each is one simple input with a timing or a read behind it. They're in the shared core (clips in `src/fighter/index.js`, frame data in `src/combat/frameData.js`, the rules in `src/combat/sim.js`), so every character has them with no character file changed.

**Getting up.** A knocked-down body lies there until its player picks (the `down` state). For the first 5 frames (`SQUASH_STEPS`) only a roll is read; after that:

| Input | What happens |
|---|---|
| nothing | he stands up by himself 1 second after touchdown (`DOWN_MAX`), so nobody can stall |
| up (held) | stands in place: the old getup, invulnerable while rising, at his hip on his own side |
| a fresh press away from him | **roll away**: 110 px back (less against a wall, down to a tuck in place), and he's still on his side |
| a fresh press toward him | **roll toward**: with the other fighter's root within 210 px of the hip (standing over the body, at its feet or just past them) and 60 px of floor behind him, it rolls **under him** and comes up 78 px past him, the one getup that changes sides; otherwise it rolls up to 80 px toward him and stops at least 130 px short, same side. With his back to the wall (no room past him) it's the short roll |
| 1 or 3 | **Wake Kick**: the knees curl up, then he springs forward along the floor onto his shoulders behind a straight kick rising into the shins of anyone standing at his feet. Mid, 8 damage, knockdown. Live about 9 frames after the press |
| 2 or 4, or down and any button | **Wake Sweep**: up on one hip, the lead leg scythes out flat along the floor. Low, 7 damage, knockdown. Live about 10 frames after the press, and it reaches a little farther |

A held direction never rolls, and away and toward go by the sides (Sides, below), so they mean the same whichever side of the screen he's on. The roll (`startRoll` and `rollStep` in `src/combat/sim.js`) first puts the body's root at its hip without moving the drawing (`rig.rebase`), so the ball turns about where the body lies and nothing jumps. The way the head points is a back roll (`rollBack`: the legs go up over the head), the way the feet point a forward roll (`rollFwd`: sit up, over the shoulder); both end crouched on the feet, he turns to face the other fighter (drawn as a turn) and stands (`rollUp`). The sim moves the body along the floor eased so it's already going its way on the first frame (half again the average speed, slowing to half), 40 steps for a roll and 48 for one crossing under him, aimed past wherever he stands now; the tumble clip is played at the rate that fills it. A roll is invulnerable and passes through bodies like any lying body, and the one crossing under him isn't stopped by him at all (`keepSides` and `keepOff` skip it). From the press to standing a roll takes about 0.6 s, standing up about 0.7.

A wake-up strike turns him to face the opponent first (a body lying with its head toward him turns over; the hip stays put), and springs the body forward 40 to 60 px, because a standing fighter can't stand closer than about 70 px to a lying body's hip from its feet side (Controls, Lying bodies). If he's standing closer than that on the head side, the body starts that much further back (`WAKE_SCOOT`), so the strike never carries the hip past him. It's invulnerable until it goes live, then it's a body on the floor until the clip's `stand` key: a hit there is a counter that puts him straight back down (and counts as that knockdown's ground hit). Then he rocks forward onto his feet through the getup's own stand-up keys, a little faster, and that rise is the price: both strikes are 17 to 24 frames minus on block (measured in getup-test), so a blocked Wake Kick (stand) or Wake Sweep (down-back) is a free punish. The strikes cost 12 stamina, which hardly matters after the knockdown refill.

The mixup runs both ways. The man standing over the body has to guess the height of a wake-up strike, or whether one is coming at all (a standing guard walks him back out of the sweep's reach, a crouching guard eats the kick), while the man on the floor chooses when to move as well as what.

**Sides.** Which side of the other fighter a body on the floor is on is fixed the moment it lands, by its hip (a launch, a throw or a cross-up may have carried it over him; `lieSide()`), and kept in both fighters' `home`, the same field that stops a standing man walking out the far side of a body. Two things change it: a roll that crosses under him, and him jumping over the body. Standing up, a wake-up strike, the 1-second stand and a roll away all rise on that side, facing him; the separation that parts a rising body from a man standing on it pushes each one toward his own side.

*Botches.* A press thrown in the middle of a wake-up strike, outside the last `BOTCH_GRACE` steps of its rise, is simply lost: there's no standing body to tangle. Presses made in the air on the way down are thrown away at touchdown, so a getup choice is always made on the floor.

*CPU.* Once per knockdown it plans a getup (`wakePlan` in `src/combat/ai.js`): it lies still for a random beat (longer and more varied the better it is), then stands, rolls away or toward, or wakes with a strike if he's standing close; the bottom of the ladder often just lies there until the clock stands it up. Away and toward go by the sides like a player's (`home`), and with the wall behind it and you standing near, a roll is toward you: under you, the way out of the corner. A strike starting at the body, or a whiff left open over it, changes the plan: a wake-up strike through it (more often the better the CPU) or a roll away (toward, under you, against the wall).

**Hitting a body on the floor.** A strike lands on a body lying on the floor only if its drawn limb touches the drawn body: the striking segment (elbow to fist, knee to foot) against the body's real lying joints drawn at their widths, within 3 px (`FLOOR_TOUCH`, `segGap()` in `src/combat/sim.js`), not the padded hurtboxes. So whether it lands can be read off the screen. What happens then:

- **A low** (frame data `height: 'low'`: the low jab, the low kick, the sweep, the characters' lows) that touches is that knockdown's **ground hit**: 55% of its damage (`GROUND.share`; the low kick does 3 to TEMP, the low jab 3, the sweep 5), counted in the combo it ends (so the decay and the soft cap apply), a 4-frame freeze with the hit spark and sound, and a jolt through the body (the hit reaction layered on the lying pose, a short crunch, and a 6 px pop). It can cancel into a jab or a cross for a meaty, as the old pokes did.
- **The Stomp** is still the best of them: down and 4 while the opponent lies on the floor within 175 px of your root (`stompOK`). Down and 3 there is the low kick (the stomp used to take both kicks, so no low kick ever reached a body).
- **Anything else that touches it** grazes: a high or a mid (the axe kick comes down on the shins), or any strike once that knockdown's ground hit is used. A graze is a 2-frame freeze, a 3 px flick, a small spark and a tap: no damage, no combo, no stamina refund, no cancel (`graze()`, the `graze` event). A strike that doesn't touch passes over as before.

Where each one reaches: the attacker always ends up at a body's feet (it lands feet toward whoever knocked it down), where the low kick and the stomp touch it; the low jab's fist is at crouch height, over the shins, so it touches only from the head side (over the body after a jump, or a body that landed past you); the sweep's foot falls about 30 px short of the feet from outside the stomp's range. The stomp: the rear knee comes up to the chest and the heel drives down onto the body as he steps in over the legs (a short `travel` that's allowed past the point where a standing body otherwise stops, though never past the hip). 9 damage, scaled by the character's damage and counted in the combo it ends, live about 10 frames after the press.

The mind game is in its recovery:

- **On hit** the body is pinned under the heel for 22 frames (`pin`, the `downPin` unit field), timed so the stomper is free a few frames before the fastest wake-up strike can land. Guard and the Wake Kick is blocked (and its rise punished); keep pressing buttons and it lands on you.
- **On a whiff** (a roll, a wake-up strike rising through its startup, a body that already stood up) it's about 25 frames of open recovery, and a wake-up strike thrown into its startup is a counter hit.
- **No loops:** every ground hit, the stomp and the lows alike, counts once per knockdown (`otgHit`), and the stomp can't be cancelled: a press in its recovery botches like any other. A body hit out of a wake-up strike counts as having taken its ground hit too. After that a low or a second stomp that touches the body only grazes it.

*CPU.* Over a body that stays down it stomps (more often the better it is), now and then low-kicks the feet (its ground hit), or waits over it with its guard up. After a stomp lands it guards the wake-up strike (20% at the bottom of the ladder, 80% at the top).

`?scene=stomp-test`: the stomp on a body that stays down, a greedy stomp counter-hit by a wake kick rising through it, a stomp whiffing on a roll, a stomp that lands followed by an instant wake kick (guarded, then not guarded and punished), and a second stomp passing over the body.

**In the air.** Three ways to act while falling, each with a timing to learn:

- **Air recovery.** Juggled or launched into the air, press any button once 18 frames have passed since the last hit (`AIR_TECH` in `src/combat/frameData.js`) and the body snaps out of the tumble into a tuck, rolls upright and lands on its feet with a short absorb (`airTech`, then `airLand`), facing the opponent, with a RECOVER callout. In the first 5 frames of that window it comes with a jump jab (RECOVER · JAB), so the timing is the skill. Each juggle hit, wall bounce and ground bounce restarts the wait, so a juggle that keeps going (the d+2 into jabs, Sky Spike, Neon Tornado) still connects. A press before the window locks the recovery out for 12 frames, and every press while locked keeps it locked, so mashing through a juggle never recovers. No recovery out of a wall splat, a throw, a floor break's fall down the stairwell, or your own trip or tangle.
- **Attack on a fall.** Dropping off the vault after a floor break (the clip's drop), a press is an air attack on the way down: the body lets go of the vault where it is and swings (`vaultFall`). The landing works like any air attack's. After an air recovery the usual one air attack is there too.
- **A second air attack.** One per jump is now two: once an air attack has recovered without touching anything, a press on the way down is a quick jump jab. A press while the first is still swinging is still an air tangle (Botches), and one that connected or was blocked keeps the old rule: the press waits for the landing and starts the ground string.

*CPU.* Juggled, it recovers more often the better it is (never at the bottom of the ladder) and closer to the jab window; after a whiffed air attack it throws the second jab when you're close; off the vault it swings at you below.

`?scene=air-recovery-test`: a launch with no press, a press too early (locked out), a press as the window opens (the jab), a later one, mashing, a juggle that connects before the window opens, the second air jab landing after a whiffed kick, a press mid-swing tangling up, and an air roundhouse out of the vault.

**The parry.** Every character can parry by **tapping** toward the opponent within 6 frames (`PARRY.win`, 12 steps, `src/combat/frameData.js`) before a strike lands: no damage, the PARRY flash and sound (the same event HELPDESK's stance uses), both freeze for 8 frames, and the striker reels for 16 frames (his string and anything buffered end), so a jab or cross punishes. Mistime it and you're just hit, because the tap moved you forward instead of guarding. The rules (`uParry` in `src/combat/sim.js`):

- **A tap, never a hold:** only a fresh press of forward counts, from idle, walking, crouching or a raised guard, on your feet and not fainted. It starts a 20-frame lockout (`PARRY.lock`) in which another tap doesn't count and restarts the lockout, so mashing forward never parries, and neither does the second tap of a forward dash. A tap within 12 frames of a button press doesn't count either (`PARRY.quiet`), so a hand mashing buttons and directions never parries by accident (the first build without this rule gave a random masher one parry every three matches).
- **Heights:** a standing tap parries highs, mids, overheads and air attacks (a jump-in is parried standing). Lows need the tap with down held (down-forward), and down-forward parries only lows, so it's a read either way.
- **Never a throw**, a trade, or a hit on a body that's already reeling, floored or in the air.
- **One strike per tap:** a multi-hit's next hit needs another tap, which the lockout won't give it, so PERFORMANCE REVIEW's hooks get one parried at most.
- HELPDESK's Ticket Closed is unchanged and much stronger: a stance that catches anything high or mid for its whole startup and hold, stuns for 46 frames and answers with its own reversal.
- *Botches:* a tap is movement, not a press, so it never botches; a tap during your own strike simply doesn't count.

*CPU.* Only above difficulty 0.55 (JANITOR up): it waits until a strike it saw coming (see the CPU's reaction time under Arcade flow) is about to go live and taps toward it (down-forward for a low), for up to 31% of strikes at difficulty 1.0 and 30% for the showcase. A strike faster than its reaction time can't be parried by it at all. The bottom of the ladder never parries.

`?scene=parry-test`: a jab and a snap kick parried and punished with the cross; a tap too early, and holding forward, getting hit; a low hitting a standing tap and parried down-forward; a jump-in parried standing; a throw going through the tap; mashing forward getting hit.

**Measurements** (the four fundamentals, against the build before them). A random masher (P1 TEMP: a random button every 3 to 9 steps, a third of them with forward, some with back, down or up) against a TEMP CPU, three matches at each difficulty, before and after: it lost every round both times (0-6 at CPU 0.3 and 0.7), dealt 56 and 98 damage before and 87 and 102 after, its best combo was 1 and 5 before and 2 and 2 after, and it spent 66% and 65% of the fight open (botching, fainted or on the floor) before, 67% and 62% after. It parried nothing by accident, recovered in the air once per three matches, and threw 5 and 8 wake-up strikes (mashing on the floor is a getup choice). CPU vs CPU at 0.7, 12 matches a pairing with sides swapped every other match, rounds won before / after: TEMP v TEMP 20-10 / 13-17, TEMP v COURIER 18-8 / 20-7, SECURITY v HELPDESK 10-21 / 12-18, MANAGER v INTERN 22-2 / 22-7, JANITOR v WINDOW WASHER 18-12 / 15-13. Twelve-match blocks swing hard (the mirror match came out 20-10), so read these as no matchup moving outside the noise. Rounds run a little longer, 15 to 20 seconds of fighting a round against 14 to 18 before, because getting up is now a decision. The new mechanics get used constantly: in those 60 matches, 292 wake-up strikes (93 Wake Kicks and 65 Wake Sweeps landed), 68 stomps landed, 60 air recoveries, 68 universal parries and 38 attacks on a fall. Every scripted scene runs with no console error (all 216 phases of the test timelines, and every scene rendered for 15 seconds), and the old scenes still show what they're named for. Their numbers shift only where a fighter is knocked down and now lies there until the clock stands him up (the scripted P2s press nothing). Save and restore: see Multiplayer, Proof for the fundamentals.

`?scene=getup-test` shows each option: lying until the clock stands him up, up, a roll away with him standing over the body, a roll toward him there (under him to his other side), a roll toward him from across the room (short, same side), a roll away with nobody near, a roll away into the wall (a tuck in place), a roll toward him with his back to the wall (no room past him: short, same side), the Wake Kick and the Wake Sweep landing on a man standing at the feet, and both blocked (standing and down-back) and punished on the rise. `?scene=ground-test` shows each ground hit: the low jab from the head side and the low kick at the feet (the first lands, the second grazes), the stomp then a low kick (the kick grazes), the sweep from outside the stomp falling short, and the axe kick grazing.

**Measurements (getups and ground hits, 2026-11-09).** Side swaps (`node gauntlet/measure.mjs swaps`): CPU vs CPU at 0.7, twelve pairings across all eighteen characters, three seeds, 90 s each, about 145,000 fight frames on each build, counted the way Measurements (side swaps) counts them (by where each body is, a lying, rolling or rising one by its hip; 10 px clear, held 8 frames). Swaps with either fighter on the floor, rolling or rising in the 30 frames before, and no roll crossing under him: 73 on main, 1 now (a stomp from point-blank right after a trade). There are 41 deliberate ones, rolls crossing under him (42 of the 125 rolls the CPUs threw crossed). What made the 73: a man standing up to 14 px past a lying hip, so every way up looked like a swap; a wake-up strike's scoot carrying the hip past a man on the head side; stale sides after a launch or throw carried the body over him; a roll re-posing the lying body about its root, a body-length jump of the hip. Every scripted test scene against main, phase by phase (`measure.mjs scene`, 33 scenes): every phase that exists on both builds gives the same best combo, damage and faint, apart from the renamed ones. Mashers (`measure.mjs mash`, 20 matches a row, main / now): random at CPU 0.3 won 0 / 0 rounds of 40, damage 1078 / 958; at 0.7 0 / 0, 1022 / 1035; mashing 1 at 0.3 3 / 4 rounds, 2920 / 3146; at 0.7 2 / 2, 2441 / 2379. A new down masher (`--kinds down`: walks in holding down, a random button every 10 to 18 steps, so it lands every ground hit it can) at 0.7 over 40 matches: 0 / 0 rounds of 80, damage 2559 / 2718, ground hits 9 / 22 (27 / 64 damage). Its best combo was 15: wake sweeps trading on the floor, which chain because a hit on a body rising out of a wake-up strike always continues the attacker's combo (already the case on main), and one low kick in it. The netsim proof (`npm run netsim:proof`): 1,933 rollbacks, 16,708 restored steps, 0 differed; 24 resyncs, 0 differed.

**Dashes** (2026-11-11; the owner: "one of my favorite things in Tekken was d+1, ws3"). A double tap is a dash: toward the opponent a burst forward, away a hop back. The numbers are `DASH` in `src/combat/frameData.js`, the rules `edges()`, `startDash()` and the `dash` state in `src/combat/sim.js`, the clips `dashF` and `dashB` in `src/combat/dash.js`.

| | Forward dash (→→) | Back dash (←←) |
|---|---|---|
| ground | about 104 px in 0.2 s (TEMP; times the character's `dash` stat) | about 61 px in 0.22 s |
| burst, then recovery | 14 steps at 660 px/s, then 10 braking | 12 steps at 420 px/s, then 14 braking |
| stamina | 6 | 8 |
| guard | none until it's over (24 steps, 12 frames) | none until it's over (26 steps, 13 frames) |
| what cuts it | any strike, at any point: the dash attacks | nothing. A button waits in the buffer for the recovery |
| the second tap held | the run, from the end of the burst | walking back, after the recovery |

- **The back dash is vulnerable the whole way.** It has no guard and no invulnerable frames from the tap until it lands and recovers, 13 frames in all. What it buys is distance: 61 px takes TEMP from the pocket (90 px) to 151 px, past a jab (reach 100), a snap kick (104) and a cross (128). A lunge cross (146, and it steps), any dash attack, a slide or a charge still reaches, and with your back to the wall it goes nowhere. In `dash-test` a lunge cross thrown as he hops hits him clean for 11.
- **No back-dash attacks.** A button pressed during a back dash isn't thrown out of it: it waits for the recovery like any buffered press. An attack out of a retreat would make the hop a way to leave and swing with one input, which is what "a commitment, not a free escape" rules out, and it would hand a masher ←← plus a button. The payoff is the whiff punish you time yourself: in `dash-test` the back dash makes a jab whiff and a dash back in with the bullet jab lands in its recovery.
- **Holding the second → runs.** When the forward burst ends with forward still held, the dash becomes the run with no recovery, so tap-tap-hold runs as it always did (14 steps later than before, behind a faster start). Shift and R2 run at once, with no dash.
- **What a double tap is** (the pillars: nothing held or repeated may trigger anything by accident). Two fresh presses of one direction within 30 steps (0.25 s, the old run window). A held direction is one press, so holding back to guard never dashes. A third press within 30 steps of the last is a mash: it counts for nothing, and neither does any press after it until the stick has rested 30 steps. So mashing a direction gets exactly one dash at the start and then walks in little stop-start steps, at 101 to 109 px/s where plain walking is 141. A double tap within 24 steps of a button press doesn't count either (the parry's `quiet` rule), so mashing buttons and a direction together gets no dash at all. Neither dash comes out fainted, rooted, crouching, in a strike or in hit- or blockstun. The first tap toward him can still parry; the second one never does (the parry's own lockout), and in `dash-test` a second tap timed exactly where a parry would be dashes him into the jab.
- **Dash-spam loses**, measured over 5 seconds from a full bar. Forward dashes at the fastest rhythm the count allows (one every 39 steps): 334 px/s, behind the run's 399, and 58 stamina gone. Back dashes at that rhythm: 202 px/s and the bar down from 100 to 12 after 15 hops, where the next one won't come out. A fighter running after him closes at about 200 px/s, and every hop is 13 frames with no guard. One beat slower (every 45 steps) the bar holds (77 after 5 s) but he covers 175 px/s, barely more than walking back, which guards.
- **Touch.** The sim ignores a touch stick's own double taps, as it always has (a thumb resting on the edge of the dead zone wobbles across it). The touch layer decides the dash instead (`flick()` in `src/touch.js`): the stick goes out left or right, comes back to the middle or the thumb lifts within 220 ms, and goes out the same way again within 320 ms of the first. The second flick holds the input's `dash` bit, which the sim reads with the direction as the double tap, under the same count. One push still walks, the rim still runs, and a forward flick held on after the dash runs. Not checked on a real phone.
- **Gamepad.** The stick and the D-pad double-tap exactly like the keyboard.
- **Online.** A dash is sim state decided from the inputs both machines share: the unit fields `dashDir`, `dashT`, `tapDir`, `tapAt` and `tapN` (the tap count replaces the old `tapTimer`), all in the snapshot and the state hash, and the input is 12 bits (`dash` is the new one). `NET_VERSION` is 24.

**Dash attacks.** Every button has a shared move out of a forward dash or a run (any point of the dash, or with forward held up to 14 steps after either). A character's own `motion: 'ff'` command still wins its button, so COURIER keeps Express Slide on 3 and Drop-Off Knee on 4 and gets the shared ones on 1 and 2, and INTERN and SALES REP keep theirs on 1. Frames are 60ths of a second from the press to contact and who is free first, measured in `dash-test` on TEMP (as a reference the jab is 4 frames and -16.5 on block, bounced out to 162 px; the cross 11 and -18.5).

| Input | Move | Height | Damage | To contact | On hit | On block | For | Its weakness |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| →→1 | BULLET JAB | high | 7 | 5.5 | +2 | -6.5, left 74 px away | the fast way in: live almost as soon as a jab, with the dash's ground under it | a crouch ducks it, and it's the one strike that isn't bounced off a guard (`blockPush: 0`), so blocked he stands in jab range: a jab punishes |
| →→2 | COMET CROSS | mid | 16 | 8.5 | knockdown, wall splat | -14.5 | the big one, unchanged | 30 stamina, and a long recovery |
| →→3 | SKID KICK | low | 8 | 7 to 8 | +4.5 | -20 | the low, for a standing guard | no knockdown, and blocked low it has no blockstun at all: the whole recovery is open (a cross punishes). A guard walking back can walk out of it |
| →→4 | RUNNING BOOT | mid | 13 | 8 to 9 | knockdown, a long shove | -11.5 | the mid: beats a crouch and knocks down | the slowest out and by far the slowest back, hit, blocked or whiffed (a cross punishes); 24 stamina |

**Rising attacks.** Letting go of down out of a crouch gives 24 steps (0.2 s, `RISE_STEPS`, unchanged) in which a button is a rising attack, and now every limb has one. A character's own `motion: 'rise'` command still wins its button.

| Input | Move | Height | Damage | To contact | On hit | On block | For | Its weakness |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| release ↓, 1 | RISING JAB | mid | 7 | 4 | +3.5 | -10.5 | the quick one out of a crouch, and a mid where the standing jab is a high | small damage, punished on block, and a full recovery on a whiff |
| release ↓, 2 | RISING UPPERCUT | mid | 11 | 6 | launch | -4.5 | unchanged | |
| release ↓, 3 | RISING KICK | mid | 11 | 4.5 | +6 | -12 (a cross punishes) | the lead kick out of the rise | 22 stamina and the longest block punish of the four |
| release ↓, 4 | RISING KNEE | mid | 12 | 2.5 to 3 | launch | -3 | unchanged | |

- **↓1, release ↓, 3 is a string: RISE AND SHINE** (`RISE_STRING` in `src/combat/dash.js`). The crouch jab, down let go, then 3 in the jab's cancel window: the rising kick comes out as the string's next hit, a true 2-hit combo for 16 when the jab lands (`dash-test`). Everyone has it; INTERN's opens with his own ↓1, Paper Cut.
- **Rising out of a crouching attack.** The rise used to be read only when a standing crouch ended. Now letting go of down in the middle of a crouching attack thrown with down held (the low jab, the low kick, the sweep, or a character's own ↓ + button in their place, like INTERN's Paper Cut; never a special on another motion, an ender or a slide, so ELECTRICIAN's Ground Fault into the cross is what it was) counts as rising for the rest of that attack, and when it ends with down let go the 24 steps start from there. That's what lets the 3 be pressed during the jab.
- **In the string table** a rising press is its own token, `r3` (a button pressed while rising), beside `3` and `d3`. A plain token after the first still matches its rising form, so GROUND RUSH's last 4 with down let go is still the string's knee. A rising press that opens nothing is its own first token, so a rising uppercut no longer counts as the 2 that opens CROSS SNAP.
- **Botches.** Nothing new is exempt. The five moves are ordinary strikes, so a press in the middle of one that isn't a string's next hit botches. Keep down held on RISE AND SHINE's 3 and it's a low kick mistimed into the jab: a botch (`dash-test`). A press during a forward dash is the dash attack and can't botch (a dash isn't a strike); a press during a back dash waits.
- **The rise isn't free.** A rising jab or kick that whiffs or is blocked has its whole recovery (28 and 35 steps after contact), and 30 steps after down is let go a 1 is the standing jab again (`dash-test`).

*CPU.* It sends the double tap as the `dash` bit. How often it thinks of a dash at all goes with difficulty: 3% of its chances at INTERN's 0.2, 12% at HELPDESK's 0.32, 28% at SECURITY's 0.55, 45% at THE MANAGER's 0.8, half for the showcase (`dashP()` in `src/combat/ai.js`). On an approach from 150 to 212 px it may dash in with the attack that fits (the skid kick on a standing guard, the running boot on a croucher, otherwise mostly the bullet jab), from further out just dash to close; in the pocket, and instead of walking back after being hit, it may hop back, never with the wall close behind it or under 34 stamina. Ducking a high from a step outside the launchers' reach it comes up with the rising kick or jab (never under 0.3, 40% at 0.8), and a low jab that lands may go on into RISE AND SHINE through the same follow-up reflex that carries every string.

**Measurements (dashes, 2026-11-11).** Main, then this build.
- **Scripted scenes** (`node gauntlet/measure.mjs scenes`, and again with `--root` on a checkout of main; a digest of both bodies and every joint on every frame). 259 of the 272 phases that exist on both builds are identical frame for frame, and dash-test adds 24. The 13 that aren't: the five CPU fights (ai-fight, ai-fight-2, ai-fight-3, horde-ai, ko), because the CPU rolls for a dash now, which moves every roll after it. string-test, whose script walks in by flicking the stick every other step: the second flick is a dash now, and the hits and the damage are the same. parry-test's mashed-forward phase and its jump-in phase, where the scripted taps come twice inside 30 steps: the second is a dash, and the outcome (hit; parried and punished) is the same. faint-test's one-direction mash: after the faint he walks where the old double tap ran him, since a mash is no dash. And four accident-test phases (the helicopter and the spring uppercut pressed a step early and late): the botching press is a 4 or a 2 with down let go in the middle of a sweep or a low kick, which is a rising press now, so the half-formed limb is the rising move's; they botch exactly as before.
- **Mashers** (`node gauntlet/measure.mjs mash`, TEMP against a TEMP CPU; rounds the masher won, his damage). Six matches a row: random mashing at CPU 0.2 0 / 0 rounds, 289 / 435 damage; at 0.3 0 / 0, 241 / 344; at 0.7 0 / 0, 248 / 231. Mashing 1 at 0.2 1 / 1 round, 921 / 829; at 0.3 0 / 0, 697 / 726; at 0.7 0 / 0, 532 / 636. The random masher's two low rows looked high, so they were run longer: at 0.3 over 20 matches 0 / 0 rounds and 938 / 955 damage; at 0.2 over 40 matches 0 of 80 / 1 of 81 rounds and 2321 / 2475 damage (+7%), open 54.3% / 54.4% of the fight. That one round is the only one a random masher took in any of these runs on this build, against the CPU that barely fights back. The masher's own inputs can't dash (its stick is re-rolled every 40 steps, and the 1-masher holds forward) and never hold down, so none of the new moves is in its hands; what moved is the CPU's dice.
- **CPU vs CPU at 0.7** (`node gauntlet/measure.mjs cpu`, 24 matches a pairing, sides swapped every match; rounds, main / now). TEMP v TEMP 31-28 / 28-31, TEMP v COURIER 34-24 / 39-18, SECURITY v HELPDESK 28-35 / 21-38, THE MANAGER v INTERN 22-33 / 25-34. A 24-match block swings by ten points or so (see the earlier measurements), and the two that moved most moved in opposite directions for the dash-heavy side: read these as no pairing clearly outside the noise, and get a longer run before retuning a character on them.
- **Rollback.** `npm run netsim:proof`, which now ends with a dash drill (two scripted hands that double-tap both ways, press in the dash, send the dash bit, and crouch, jab and rise into the kick) and saves more often inside a dash: 2,405 rollbacks, 95 of them saved inside a dash, 0 of 20,755 restored steps differed; 30 resyncs, 0 of 7,200 steps differed.
- **Not measured:** the touch stick's double flick on a real phone (it's logic in `src/touch.js` that no script here drives), and a person's read on whether the dash distances feel right.

### How a KO resolves (2026-11-11)

One place decides what a KO means. Before this, each hit path in `applyHit` decided on its own: at 0 health it called a 1v1 KO, only the plain hit knew about horde, and three paths that didn't ended horde runs (Arcade flow, Horde). Now it's split three ways, all in `src/combat/sim.js`:

- **Hit paths deal damage and react.** Every branch (plain hit and throw, juggle, wake-up counter, armor) lowers health, plays its reaction and sends its `hit` event with `ko: true` where it applies. At 0 health it calls `koBody()`, which shapes the fall (the KO pose, the launch, the ragdoll) and reports the KO. No hit path ends a round, touches `sim.phase` or `sim.winner`, or calls `goonDown()`. The stomp, ground hits and floor or stair falls stop at 1 health, and statuses and clashes do no damage.
- **`resolveKOs()` decides.** It takes the KOs reported this step in the order they happened and hands each to the mode's `onKO`. It runs at fixed points in `tick()`: right after `resolveHits()`, and at the start of each unit's update. The second one is there because a throw lands inside the unit loop, and the units after it have to see the round over on that same step: their stamina settle and their CPU both read `sim.phase`, and resolving later would change 1v1. After `resolveHits()` it also sweeps for anyone at 0 health that no path reported, by unit index, logs a warning and resolves them like any KO. So a new damage path that forgets `koBody()` still ends in a KO the rules decide, never in someone standing at 0 health. The pending list is always empty when a step ends, so it's never part of a snapshot.
- **The mode rules table**, `RULES.versus` and `RULES.horde`, read through `rules()` (and `sim.rules()`): `onKO` (versus: any KO ends the round; horde: a goon goes down `goonKO()`, the hero ends the run), `endsRound`, `clock`, `oneRound`, `floorBreak`, `regroup`, `followPair` (the fight's floor follows the pair), `perfect`, `dance`, `timeByPoints`, `hitstunFrom` (a goon's hit stuns half as long) and `matchBanner`. A check that's about the mode's rules reads `rules()`. One that's about horde state (the wave, the floor, the transit, a goon's health from the run's config) still reads `sim.horde`. `sim.js` branched on `sim.horde` in 16 places before this and does in 3 now: the `rules()` switch and those two pieces of state. The flow went from 1 to none.
