When two members are ready, the one who pressed first hosts: the crew room claims a quick room code and tells each page its role, and both go to the pick screen (VS BOB, opening on each one's last pick in this crew, else the favorite) and then the match, all on the usual quick-room machinery (`online.js` with `pickFirst`). After each match the post-match screen works as always (rematch countdowns, NEW CHARACTERS, the host's final call); its LEAVE ROOM reads BACK TO CREW, and the other player's notice says ALICE WENT BACK TO THE CREW with BACK TO CREW. A rival who never connects sends you back to the lobby after 20 seconds (BOB DIDN'T CONNECT).
- **Winner stays on.** A third member who presses PLAY while two are fighting waits (PLAY THE WINNER). When that match ends with a winner, the crew room tells both players: the screen says YOU STAY ON or BACK TO THE CREW, WINNER STAYS ON · CAROL IS NEXT, the rematch countdown stops, and after 4 seconds both go back to the lobby, where the winner is ready at once at the front of the line and gets paired with the one waiting. A draw changes nothing.
- **Recording.** The host's page is authoritative, as in every online match: when a match ends it sends `{ t: 'result', match, n, winner, wins, chars }` over the crew socket. The crew room accepts it only from the member it made host of that match, and counts it once, keyed by `<match id>:<n>`, so a resend or a reconnect can't count it twice. It updates both members' wins, losses, draws, rounds and last pick, their play count per fighter (the favorite is the most played), the head to head for the pair, and the recent list, then sends the new standings to everyone in the lobby. The post-match screen shows CREW RECORD VS BOB 3–2 from the server instead of this browser's record.
- **What a crew room keeps** (its Durable Object's own SQLite storage, `worker/crew.ts`): the crew's name, slug and dates; each member's display name, first and last seen, wins, losses, draws, rounds won and lost, and last pick; play counts per fighter; head to head for every pair that has played (wins each, draws, rounds each); the last 300 matches (when, who hosted, who joined, the winner, rounds, fighters; the lobby shows the latest); and the matches it started (a day). Members are stored by a hash of the player id salted with the crew's slug, never the id itself. At most 50 members (a 51st gets THIS CREW IS FULL) and 80 open sockets.
- **Expiry.** Every visit and every result pushes the crew's expiry to 90 days out and re-arms its alarm; when the alarm finds it past due and nobody connected, it deletes all of the crew's storage and its alarm. A link to a deleted (or mistyped) crew shows NO CREW AT THAT LINK with ONLINE MENU and MAIN MENU. With `DEV_HOOKS=1` (`.dev.vars` only), `POST /api/crews/<slug>/dev-expire` expires a crew at once, for testing.
- **High scores** (2026-11-11). A crew has its own ARCADE and HORDE tables, the top 10 of each, like a cabinet's: initials, score, fighter, the stage (CLEAR past the boss) or the floors, and the date. Initials are the identity on the board, never the profile name. They're the lobby's ARCADE and HORDE tabs, next to STANDINGS. **Which runs count:** a finished arcade or horde run, once its initials are in, posts to every crew on this device's recent list (the three the online menu shows) that the player is a member of (has opened the crew's link). The server checks the membership, so the page just posts to all three, and a crew you're not in says `not-member` and keeps nothing. A run with no crew on the list posts nowhere new; the local tables work as before. A run that misses this device's table but would make a crew's still gets the initials (`src/net/crewScores.js` fetches the crews' tables at GAME OVER to know), and goes on to that crew's table with the new row blinking. `POST /api/crews/<slug>/scores` takes `{ pid, run: { kind, initials, score, char, reach, clear } }` and answers `{ ok, rank }` or `{ error }`; `GET` the same path gives `{ name, arcade, horde }` for the attract loop and the initials screen, to anyone with the link, never who posted a row.
  - **Plausible, not secure.** Scores are the page's word. The crew refuses junk (`bad-score`: a kind that isn't arcade or horde, initials that aren't three of A to Z and 0 to 9, a fighter not on the roster, a score or reach that isn't a whole number, a stage outside 1 to 7, floors outside 0 to 9,999), a score over what the rules allow for how far the run got (`too-high`; `scoreCap()` in `src/flow/scorecap.js`, shared by the page and the Worker: 150,000 a stage reached plus the stage clear bonuses, and 60,000 a floor counting the one the run ended on; that's the most generous rules, best of 5 with the full clock, so no real run comes near it), more than one run per member every 20 seconds or 30 a day (`slow-down`; junk doesn't use them up), and anyone who isn't a member. On top of that, `EVENT_LIMIT` caps posts per IP (60 a minute). What it can't stop is a member making up a score under the cap; the crew can see whose initials those are.
- **Stats** (2026-11-11), the lobby's STATS tab: the crew's total time played (the summed length of its matches, as 45S, 12M, 3H 12M or 2D 4H) and matches played, the most-picked fighters, each player's time played and most-used fighter, and the crew's own matchup table, fighter against fighter, each with the left fighter's win rate within the crew (a draw is half) and the number of matches. Wins and losses stay on STANDINGS. A match's length comes from the host's result (`secs`, the frame the result showed on, at 120 Hz); matches from before this change have none, so an old crew's time starts from now. Its matchup table is filled once from the matches it still kept (the last 300) the first time the new code opens it.
- **What it adds to the crew's storage** (2026-11-11): `mtime` (each member's seconds played), `fpairs` (the crew's matchup table: each pair of fighters once with `a <= b`, matches, wins each, draws, seconds), `scores` (the two tables' rows, with which member posted each, kept to the top 10) and `posts` (when each member posted, a day's worth, for the rate limit), plus the crew's total seconds in `meta`. The lobby's `state` carries `stats` and `scores` (with the member, so a row of your own reads in pink). The tabs sit at the top of the standings box, so the upright layout ([twgdsx]'s one grid, still in flight on 2026-11-11) places them without change; checked by merging that branch locally.
- **Checked** (2026-11-11). `npm run test:crew` runs `scripts/crew/check.mjs` (37 checks, the Worker through its sockets and HTTP) then `scripts/crew/lobby.mjs` (41 checks, real pages at 1280x720, 844x390 and 390x844). A crew match end to end: two members press PLAY, the room counts it live as crew, both pages report the result, and it lands in `stats_matchups` (pvp), in `stats_time` as crew, in the crew's standings, and in its stats (time, matches, picks, the matchup table, each member's time); a resend counts nowhere twice. Scores: taken, refused within 20 s, over the cap, 12 kinds of junk, a stranger, an unknown crew; the table keeps 10. The pages: each tab shows its table or stats on screen, and a horde run that misses this device's table goes through the initials (LUNCH CREW · 2ND PLACE) to the crew's table, highlighted, and into the attract loop's turn. Fixed on the way: the crew kept an empty fighter for WINDOW WASHER, HR REP and SALES REP in a result, since its check didn't allow capitals in an id; it now checks the roster.
- **Messages** on the crew socket (JSON; `src/net/crew.js` on the page, which reconnects by itself after a drop and keeps a pending PLAY): from the page `hello {pid, name}` (first), `name`, `play {on, stay}`, `lobby` (back from a match), `result` (with `secs`, the match's length, since 2026-11-11); from the room `welcome {you}`, `state {crew, members, h2h, recent, stats, scores}` (to everyone, on every change; `members` carry `secs`, each one's time played), `match {id, code, role, rival}`, `rotate {match, stay, next}` and `error {reason}` (`no-crew`, `crew-full`, `full`, `bad-hello`, `flood`; the Worker's `busy`). Each socket gets 4 KB per message and 10 messages a second (a burst of 30).

**Profiles and sign-in.** (What's kept and for how long, the age question before the email, and the name filter: Privacy and terms.) Nobody needs an account. A player is a random 128-bit id plus a display name, kept in localStorage as `stick-fight-player` (`{ id, name, email, crews }`, `src/net/profile.js`; every storage call wrapped, so a private window just keeps it for the page). The name is the only thing other players see. The id only ever goes to our own server, which uses it to know you in each crew (hashed there); it never goes to the other player. PROFILE in the online menu shows the name, CHANGE NAME, and SAVE MY PROFILE (or SIGN OUT once saved), BACK and MAIN MENU.

- **SAVE MY PROFILE** asks the age question first (Privacy and terms; since 2026-11-11), then for an email (the on-screen keyboard has @ . - _; a paste works), then sends a 6-digit code and a one-click link from `hey@neonstickfight.com`. Typing the code (VERIFY; SEND AGAIN for a new one) links this device's player id to that email in D1. On another device, SIGN IN on the first name screen (or SAVE MY PROFILE) with the same email restores the same id, name and crew list, and with the id every crew record: WELCOME BACK, ALICE: PROFILE RESTORED. The crew lists merge. A device whose id is already saved under another email is told to sign in with that one (making it a fresh id would cut it off from its own records). SIGN OUT (press twice) ends the session and starts this device fresh with a new id, for a shared machine. DELETE MY PROFILE (press twice, on its own row) deletes the account on the server and then does the same (Privacy and terms, Deleting a profile).
- **The link** is `https://neonstickfight.com/play/#signin=<email>&code=<code>` (`worker/auth.ts`, whichever address asked; a link from before the move to play/, without `play/`, is forwarded by the homepage with its hash, and one from askscottpierce.com keeps its hash through the 301). The fragment never reaches a server log, and the page submits it from JavaScript, so a mail scanner that opens links can't use the code up. Opened in the same tab (only the fragment changes), the page notices and starts over on the code screen.
- **Protections** (`worker/auth.ts`): codes are 6 digits, last 10 minutes and take 5 tries, and only their hashes are stored (SHA-256 of the email and code); a used code is deleted, so it works once. Sends: one per email per 30 seconds, 5 per email and 20 per IP an hour. Guesses: 10 per email an hour and 20 a day, 30 per IP an hour, counted before the code is checked. All of it also sits behind `AUTH_LIMIT` (20 calls a minute per IP). Sessions are 30 days, stored as hashes, in an HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite=Lax cookie with `Path=/` on neonstickfight.com (`Path=/stick-fight` on the old address while it serves, since that domain is shared with other apps). A session from askscottpierce.com doesn't follow to the new domain: sign in again there. Requests that change something must be JSON, which a cross-site form can't send. An email is never shown to another player and only ever goes back to its signed-in owner (`GET /me`). Expected refusals (a wrong code, a cooldown) answer 200 with `{ error }`, so the page's console stays clean.
- **API:** `POST auth/start {email}`, `POST auth/verify {email, code, pid, name}` (sets the cookie), `GET me` (`{ email, pid, name, crews, prefs }` or `null`), `PUT me {name?, crews?, prefs?}`, `POST auth/logout`, `DELETE me {crews?}` (DELETE MY PROFILE), all under `/api/`. The page calls `GET me` when the online menu opens, so a session from another visit is picked up and an ended one noticed; a signed-in touch device also calls it once at start, for the prefs.
- **Prefs** are the settings that follow the account: a small JSON object in `accounts.prefs` (`migrations/0002_prefs.sql`). The server keeps only keys it knows, each checked against its range (`cleanPrefs()` in `worker/auth.ts`), and drops the rest, so a later setting is one more key there and no migration. Today there's one: `touch: { scale, at }`, the touch-control size (0.5 to 2) and when the player last changed it. The rule, on the page (`mergeTouchPref()` in `src/touchPrefs.js`) and on the server (`mergePrefs()`): a device that has never set a size takes the account's; otherwise the most recent change wins, and a device whose size is newer sends it up. A change while signed in is saved to the account 0.8 s after the last press. Signed out, the size is in localStorage alone, and SIGN OUT leaves it on the device.
- **Google sign-in** would plug in beside the code: an OAuth callback under `/api/auth/google` that, once Google vouches for the email, calls the same `linkAccount()` and `createSession()` as a verified code. It needs an OAuth client registered by the owner (Google Cloud console, redirect URI on neonstickfight.com) and its secret as a Worker secret, so it isn't built.
- **Dev mode.** With `DEV_LOGIN_CODES=1` in `.dev.vars`, no email is sent: `auth/start` returns the code, and the code screen shows DEV MODE: YOUR CODE IS 123456. Vite's dev proxy rewrites the cookie's path to `/`, because the dev server serves the site at `/` (the game at `/play/`).
- **Tested** (local Worker and Vite, Playwright, 2026-11-08): A (Chromium, keyboard) named itself, created TUESDAY CREW and got the link in the address bar; B (Firefox, mouse on the on-screen keyboard) and C (Chromium, touch) opened the link and were asked their names first; presence showed all three on all three screens. A and B played a match ended by the same scripted KO frame on both sides (`net.haltAt` to a common confirmed frame, then `debugKO` on both): no desync, and the result showed in the standings on all three screens and as CREW RECORD VS BOB 1–0 on the post-match screen. A went back to the crew and B got ALICE WENT BACK TO THE CREW. In a second match C waited, B won, and winner stays on sent A to the lobby and B and C to the pick screen. After reloading all three: same crew, names, 1–1 records, and favorites (INTERN, COURIER); match 2's pick screen opened on each one's last pick. Then A saved its profile (a wrong code: 4 TRIES LEFT; SEND AGAIN at once: WAIT 30 SECONDS), and a fourth context on a fake gamepad typed and deleted on the name screen, reached SIGN IN on the D-pad, signed in with the email link, and came back as ALICE with the same player id, the crew in its list and the 1–1 record in the lobby. With curl: the sixth crew in a minute got 429, the 19th code send from one IP in an hour got `too-many` and AUTH_LIMIT then `busy`, guesses ran out per code and per email, and the expiry hook deleted a crew. Zero console errors; `npm run typecheck` passes.

**Determinism.** Netplay that runs the sim on both machines only works if both compute the same sim to the last bit. Run the same recorded input log through the sim in Chromium, Firefox and WebKit, with the builtin `Math.sin`, `cos`, `atan2`, `acos`, `hypot` and `pow`, and the rig's joints differ in the last bit by step 6 to 40 in every engine pair (the outcome still matched over 100 seconds, but every hash check would fail and the drift can flip a close hit). The sim and the rig now use `src/combat/dmath.js` instead: fdlibm's polynomials written with only + - * / and `Math.sqrt`, which IEEE 754 makes identical on every engine. With it, all three engines produce identical hashes on every step over 6000 steps, for a scripted bout and for a CPU-vs-CPU match. A match also starts with `sim.resetMatch({ fresh: true })`, which clears every leftover of earlier play (unit fields, the rig's jump arc and fall surfaces, the reach table the sim learns from hits): without it, each player's attract demo leaked in and the first hash check already failed.

**Rollback.** `src/net/rollback.js` keeps every frame's real inputs for both players in a 1024-frame history and moves the sim only through `simulate(frame)`.

- **Guessing.** A remote input that hasn't arrived is guessed as the newest one held. Before a frame runs on a guess, the whole sim is saved (`sim.saveState()`) into a ring of reused snapshots; frames run on real inputs need no snapshot. When a real input arrives and differs from the guess used for its frame, the next `advance()` loads the snapshot from before that frame and re-runs `simulate()` up to the present.
- **Depth.** At most 12 frames (100 ms) ever run on guesses (`MAX_ROLLBACK`); past that the sim waits for the other side (a stall: WAITING FOR RIVAL after 300 ms). Time spent stalled is given up, not banked, so a side doesn't burst ahead and stall again. The side that's ahead of the other (from the frame number in each packet and half the ping) runs 10% slow, 30% past 6 frames.
- **Delay.** The local input runs 2 frames late (17 ms). When half the ping plus the jitter is longer than the rollback window covers, the delay grows one frame a second (up to 10), and a second with stalls while not ahead adds one; it comes back down with headroom.
- **Save and restore.** `src/combat/snapshot.js` (`sim.saveState(reuse?)` / `sim.loadState(s)`) captures everything that feeds the next tick: the sim's fields (phase, timers, round, wins, card, callouts, combo, the horde and its ragdolls, the measurement log), the rng's state (`mulberry32` exposes `rng.state`), the reach table the sim learns from hits, the bounds, every unit field by value (its `prevJoints` points copied), each rig through `rig.getState()` / `rig.setState()`, and the arena state `tick()` mutates (its clock, door swings, the body pile and how settled each body is). The rig's state is its closure: the move and key list, the clock inside it, the pose and its velocity, the reaction layer, the springs, the point simulation (every point), the crossfade, the jump arc and landing ease, the fall surfaces and walls. What the rig mutates in place is copied both ways; poses, keys and move defs never change, so they're shared. A restore writes into the same unit and rig objects, so events, the HUD and the camera keep valid references. Saving takes about 20 µs, loading about 20 µs.
- **Effects.** The sim's events go through `sim.eventFilter` while the netcode runs it. The first simulation of a frame fires all its events and records them by identity (type, the units involved, move, callout text; position only for events with no unit, coarsely). A re-run of that frame fires only events beyond what already fired for it, counted, so two footsteps stay two: a hit the guess missed sparks and sounds up to 100 ms late, and nothing fires twice. An effect from a wrong guess isn't taken back (a stray spark or whoosh now and then). Sound, particles, lights, shake, callouts and the flow's scoring all hang off these events, so they all follow this policy; the HUD draws the current state, so a correction shows at once.
- **Hashes.** Only confirmed frames are hashed and compared: a frame is confirmed once both real inputs are in and no correction is waiting to re-run it. Every 30th frame's hash goes out then. The hash covers the rng's state and the reach table the sim steps strings in by (only the two fighters' entries, in any key order: each machine also keeps the reach of every character its demo has shown).
- **Resync.** A mismatch at a frame past the last resync makes the host send its confirmed state: the state after its newest confirmed frame (the live sim when nothing past it has run, else the snapshot saved before the next frame, which ran on a guess), as text (`src/net/state.js`), in 12,000-character parts over the reliable room socket (the room drops messages over 16 KB). A state is 17 to 34 KB: plain values, plus references the guest already has (its units and rigs, the controllers, the character definitions and the rig's clips, which the rig compares by identity), numbers JSON can't carry (`-0`, `NaN`, infinities, `undefined`) marked, and shared objects kept shared. The guest loads it and re-runs the sim from that frame to where it was, on the inputs it has (a guest that was behind jumps ahead), in a few milliseconds. Each resync bumps an epoch: hash checks of frames before the resync point stop counting, and the host ignores the guest's hashes from an older epoch, so a stale hash in flight doesn't set off another resync. The host sends at most one every 0.7 s and the guest asks (`resync?`) at most once a second. Both show RESYNC for 1.5 s. A host that diverged is still the authority: the point is that both screens show one fight.
- **Why it desynced** (2026-11-09, found with the harness below). A character's reach table (`reachSeen`, which a string's step-in and the auto guard read) was measured on the live rig the first time that phone showed the character: mid-demo, on the stairs, ragdolled or mid-jump, and at whatever x the rig stood (the last bits of `h.x - rig.x` depend on it). Two phones that had shown different demos got different tables, and the first step-in on a cancelled string came out a few last bits apart (`u1.vx: -176.9906555925494 vs -176.99065559255004`), which could grow into a hit on one screen and a whiff on the other. `calibrate()` now runs on the rig reset to one fixed state and spot, and puts the rig's own state back. The hash didn't cover the table, so nothing caught it until positions drifted; now it does. With no recovery, any desync ran apart for the rest of the match, and a match's end decided by one side's sim moved that side to the next match while the other was still fighting: the report from the car.
- **What was checked and is fine.** The draw never touches the sim: the harness hashes the whole sim (everything `saveState()` holds) before and after the life layer's joints are swapped onto each rig, drawn and swapped back, on 30 Hz, 60 Hz, 120 Hz and uneven frames, with no difference; `render()` now puts the real joints back in a `finally`, so an exception mid-draw can't leave the drawn ones on the rig, and `rig.sway` is outside `getState()` and the hash. Nothing outside the sim moves it during a match: `main.js` now runs the flow's per-frame hooks only on its `onlineFighting` screen while a match is on (its demo screens hold a KO card longer and start a new bout after one round, which on one phone would split the fights). No `Math.random`, `Date` or object iteration order reaches the sim (the life layer's randomness is its own). The celebration dance and the CPU dwell are in the sim or the CPU only. Long dropouts: each side stalls 12 frames past the other's last input, so neither runs far ahead, the backlog never passes 64 inputs a packet, and the 1,024-frame history is never at risk (a side whose inputs did fall out of it ends the match as CONNECTION LOST instead of playing on with a hole); stalls during a dropout no longer count as a long trip, so the input delay doesn't climb a frame per second of silence; and the 15 second timeout (it was 10) rides out a 10 second dropout as WAITING FOR RIVAL.
- **Cellular conditions (the netsim harness).** `npm run netsim` (`scripts/netsim/`) runs two phones in one Node process, each with its own sim, rigs, arena, life layer and attract demo, through the real `online.js`, `rollback.js` and codec, joined over a fake room (through `online.js`'s `game.createLink` seam) on a virtual clock. The link loses and delays packets: one-way latency of 80 to 400 ms drifting by 40% over 17 s, half-normal jitter, 5 to 15% random loss plus bursts of 70% loss, and 2, 5 and 10 second dropouts in both directions; control messages are never lost but wait out a dropout. The phones run at 30, 60 or 120 Hz or uneven frames (hitches of 40 to 130 ms, doubled frames), and one is backgrounded for 3 to 8 seconds, frozen (iOS: no frames, timers or deliveries, packets to it lost) or hidden (Android: no frames). A CPU on its own rng reads each phone's sim and presses its buttons. Each session is a crew room: both pick on the pick screen, then best-of-3 matches with 30-second rounds back to back, NEW CHARACTERS now and then. Three scenarios knock one phone's sim off by a hair every 15 to 20 s and on the frame each match's result shows, to exercise the resync and the end agreement. A session fails if a match ends with different results or a different final state, or one side plays on after the other stopped. `npm run netsim -- --seeds 10 --only dropout-10s` runs one scenario; `--list` lists them. Measured 2026-11-09, each session 3 matches. Before is this branch's harness on the previous build (`NET_VERSION` 15, seeds 1 to 4); after is this build (seeds 1 to 12). A desync report is one per side per episode; "in different fights" means a match ended with a different final state on the two phones.

  | Scenario | Before: sessions, hash checks that differed, desync reports, in different fights | After: sessions, hash checks that differed, desync reports, resyncs, in different fights |
  |---|---|---|
  | `cell-80ms-5%` (60/60 Hz, draw checked) | 4, 16, 12, 0 | 12, 0, 0, 0, 0 |
  | `cell-200ms-10%` (60/30 Hz) | 4, 166, 14, 0 | 12, 0, 0, 0, 0 |
  | `cell-400ms-15%` (120/60 Hz) | 4, 404, 8, 1 | 12, 0, 0, 0, 0 |
  | `30hz-vs-120hz` (draw checked) | 4, 326, 16, 1 | 12, 0, 0, 0, 0 |
  | `uneven-frames` (draw checked) | 4, 277, 16, 2 | 12, 0, 0, 0, 0 |
  | `dropout-2s` (three of them) | 4, 31, 10, 0 | 12, 0, 0, 0, 0 |
  | `dropout-5s` | 4, 158, 12, 1 | 12, 0, 0, 0, 0 |
  | `dropout-10s` | 4, 1, 2, 0 (every session dropped as CONNECTION LOST at the first dropout) | 12, 0, 0, 0, 0 (each rides out as WAITING FOR RIVAL) |
  | `background-freeze` (iOS, 3 to 8 s) | 4, 320, 12, 1 | 12, 0, 0, 0, 0 |
  | `background-hidden` (Android) | 4, 119, 12, 0 | 12, 0, 0, 0, 0 |
  | `everything` (250 ms, 12%, dropouts, uneven v 30 Hz, hidden) | 4, 107, 12, 0 | 12, 0, 0, 0, 0 |
  | `inject-guest` (nudged every ~15 s and at the result) | 4, 1,799, 24, 4 | 12, 175, 337, 336, 0 |
  | `inject-host` | 4, 1,988, 24, 4 | 12, 189, 359, 360, 0 |
  | `inject-both-dropout` (both, 5 s dropouts) | 4, 2,473, 24, 4 | 12, 361, 678, 680, 0 |

  Totals: before, 56 sessions and 155 matches, 8,185 of 50,364 hash checks differed and 18 sessions ended a match in different fights (6 of the 44 without injected faults), and the four 10-second-dropout sessions were dropped. After, 168 sessions and 504 matches (every one finished), 165,097 hash checks: with no injected faults not one differed in 132 sessions, and the 1,255 injected faults were all put right by 1,376 resyncs; no match ended with two different results or final states, no session got stuck, and the draw never changed the sim (thousands of deep-hash checks on the draw-checked scenarios). A clean run of the whole matrix at 4 seeds takes about 20 minutes on one core; scenarios run independently, so `--only` splits it across processes.
- **Proof of the resync.** `npm run netsim:proof` (`scripts/netsim/proof.mjs`) runs the save-and-restore proof below in Node, and then, at random steps of the same bouts, encodes the state as text, loads it into a second sim that has played a different demo with different characters (so its rigs, reach table and arena all differ), and runs both on the same inputs: 8 bouts of 9,000 steps (TEMP v TEMP, HELPDESK v JANITOR, COURIER v SECURITY, WINDOW WASHER v INTERN, THE MANAGER v BARISTA, THE CEO v WELLNESS COACH, CHEF v ELECTRICIAN, RECEPTIONIST v LAWYER; 30-second rounds, across round and match ends): 3,440 rollbacks with all 29,504 restored steps matching the reference's deep hash, and 48 states sent as text (29 KB on average, 36 KB at most), each loaded on the other sim and then identical for all 11,520 steps checked after.
- **Proof of the save and restore.** A recorded CPU-vs-CPU bout (two showcase CPUs, their inputs and rng recorded, 14,000 steps each over six seeds, including round and match ends) was replayed with about 5,500 rollbacks: at random frames and, more often, at frames in hitstop, throws, wall splats, juggles, ragdolls, KOs, floor breaks, the stair tumble, the follow-down vault, getups and match ends, save, run 1 to 16 steps, restore, run again, restore, run again. A deep hash of every sim field, every unit field and the rig's whole `getState()` matched the reference run on all 132,000 checked steps; leaving out the rig, the rng or the arena clock from the restore fails it at once. The stamina faint and combo decay (sim behavior, so `NET_VERSION` is 3) keep their state in plain unit fields (`faint`, `faintDmg`, `faintAt`, `comboDmg`, `selfChain`, `lastHitAt`), which the snapshot copies with every other unit field; a save, run 480 to 900 steps, restore and replay of the recorded inputs (and the CPU's rng state after each of its calls) matched the full snapshot on every step, saved mid-faint, mid-combo and before either, and knocking one step off the restored faint clock made it differ on the first step.
- **Match rules online** (`NET_VERSION` 13). The host's round time and round wins ride every `start` message as `rules: { roundTime, winsNeeded }` (`game.rulesSetting()`); the guest takes only values it knows (`cleanRules()`, anything else is the default), and both sims set `sim.roundTime` and `sim.winsNeeded` before the match, so both play the same rules. They and `sim.roundClock` are in the snapshot (`SIM_SCALARS`) and the state hash.
- **Proof for the floor** (`NET_VERSION` 19: the drawn radii in the ground snap and the point simulation, the nosing line, the risers, the poses fitted to the treads). `npm run netsim:proof`: 1,620 rollbacks, all 13,956 restored steps matched the reference, and 24 states sent as text and loaded into a second sim stayed identical for all 5,760 steps checked after. The point simulation's new per-step field (`ox`, `oy`: where a joint was at the start of the step) is written before it's read in every step, so it isn't state.
- **Proof for the stairs** (`NET_VERSION` 15: the wide building, walkable flights, floor breaks over either rail). Four CPU-vs-CPU bouts at difficulty 0.9 (TEMP v TEMP, HELPDESK v JANITOR, COURIER v SECURITY, WINDOW WASHER v INTERN), 9,000 steps each across match ends, every odd round started with P2 on the lower floor so the bouts keep using the stairs, recorded with their inputs, the rng state after each CPU call and `aaJump`, `grabId`, `rollId` (keyed by tick), and replayed identically. The bouts held about 18,500 fighter-steps on a flight, 3,800 of them in a fight on the steps, 1,100 lying on the steps, 765 falling down a stairwell and 450 vaulting. Then 2,723 rollbacks of 1 to 16 steps (save, run, restore, run again), 1,819 of them saved with someone on a flight, falling or vaulting: all 23,072 restored steps matched a deep hash of `saveState()`, and the replay with its rollbacks matched the recording on every step. Restoring with a fighter moved to the other flight (`u.stair`) made 798 of 800 checks differ. The new state is plain unit fields (`stair`, `fallWell`, `vaultWell`) and the rig's `slopes` (in `getState()`), so the snapshot copies it with no change; the desync hash (`src/net/hash.js`) now covers `stair`.
- **Proof for the fundamentals** (`NET_VERSION` 12: the getup options, the wake-up strikes, the stomp, air recovery, falling attacks and the parry). Four CPU-vs-CPU bouts at difficulty 0.9 (TEMP v TEMP, HELPDESK v JANITOR, COURIER v SECURITY, WINDOW WASHER v INTERN), 9,000 steps each across match ends, recorded with their inputs, the rng state after each CPU call and `aaJump`, `grabId`, `rollId` (the recorders registered as script controllers, and the recording keyed by tick, since `stepCount` restarts every match), replayed identically. Then 1,978 rollbacks of 1 to 16 steps (save, run, restore, run again), 1,059 of them with the save point inside a new state (lying down, pinned under a stomp, a wake-up strike or a stomp in progress, a body in the air that can recover, an air recovery's fall, a parry's reel or a fresh tap's window, a second air attack): all 16,617 restored steps matched the reference's deep hash of `saveState()`. The bouts held 32 wake-up strikes, 12 parries, 11 air recoveries, 4 stomps landed and 2 attacks on a fall. Nudging `downPin`, `wakeUp`, `techLock` and `parryAt` on restore made 3,103 of 5,112 restored steps differ. The new per-fighter state is plain unit fields (`downPin`, `wakeUp`, `techLock`, `parryAt`, `parryLow`, `parryLock`, `pressAt`), so the snapshot copies it with no change.
- **Proof for characters and the back-walk** (`NET_VERSION` 5). Seven CPU-vs-CPU bouts at difficulty 0.9 (COURIER vs SECURITY three times, SECURITY vs TEMP twice, COURIER vs TEMP twice; about 56,000 steps) were recorded with their inputs, the rng state after each CPU call and the unit fields the CPU writes (`aaJump`, `grabId`, `rollId`), then replayed with 1,616 rollbacks: save, run 1 to 16 steps, restore, run again, comparing a deep hash of the whole `saveState()` (every sim field, every unit field, every rig's `getState()`) with the reference on each step. Half the frames inside a unique move (Express Slide, Drop-Off Knee, SAME DAY, Escort and its grab, Shoulder Check and its armor, LOCKDOWN) were save points, plus back-walks, blocks, blockstun, ragdolls and random frames. All 13,662 restored steps (and the 42,000 plain replay steps) matched. Restoring with `armorLeft` zeroed, the travel `glide` dropped, or the unit's character swapped made it differ on the first step. The same three pairings, run 6,000 steps from one seed, give the same hash on every step in Chromium, Firefox and WebKit. Two pages through the local room Worker started an online match from the handshake with COURIER (host) vs SECURITY (guest) on both, with matching confirmed hashes.
- **Cost.** The worst case, every rendered frame restoring the state from 8 frames back and re-running 8 frames (saving each), over a 12,000-step bout on an M-series Mac: Chromium 0.35 ms per rendered frame on average (p99 0.9 ms, one 5.7 ms garbage-collection spike), WebKit 0.18 ms, Firefox 0.23 ms, Node 0.34 ms (max 1.5 ms). At 12 frames, Chromium 0.41 ms average, 1.4 ms max. A plain step is 16 to 36 µs. In the network tests below the netcode's whole `advance()` averaged 0.2 to 0.4 ms per frame, and the longest single correction took 2 ms.
- **Network tests.** Two browsers of different engines played 3,600 frames (30 s) of scripted, human-paced input through the local Worker with `?netsim=` on both pages, peer to peer (Chromium and Firefox) and through the forced relay (`?relay=1`, Chromium and WebKit). Every confirmed hash (120 checks a side) and the final hash matched on both sides in all eight runs. Rollbacks are per side over the 30 s; depth in frames of 8.3 ms.

  | Added RTT, jitter, loss | Path | Measured RTT | Delay | Rollbacks | Avg / max depth | Stalls |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | 0 | P2P | 11–20 ms | 2 | 1, 6 | 1.0–1.3 / 2 | 0 |
  | 0 | relay | 21–26 ms | 2 | 24, 0 | 1.1 / 2 | 0 |
  | 60 ms, ±8, 2% | P2P | 127–143 ms | 3 | 110, 316 | 5.5, 4.6 / 12 | 43, 0 |
  | 60 ms, ±8, 2% | relay | 91–106 ms | 2 | 307, 230 | 3.9, 1.9 / 11 | 0 |
  | 120 ms, ±15, 3% | P2P | 165–181 ms | 5–10 | 101, 309 | 5.9, 5.6 / 12 | 57, 8 |
  | 120 ms, ±15, 3% | relay | 149–160 ms | 2 | 314, 320 | 6.1, 5.4 / 12 | 2, 0 |
  | 200 ms, ±25, 5% | P2P | 281 ms | 10 | 118, 313 | 6.9, 7.8 / 12 | 73, 35 |
  | 200 ms, ±25, 5% | relay | 226–230 ms | 6–7 | 302, 324 | 6.1 / 12 | 14, 1 |

  The P2P runs measure 60 to 80 ms more round trip than was added, with 30 to 45 ms of jitter: headless Firefox's DataChannel delivery, not the netcode (the relay runs, through the same Worker, measure close to what was added). Past about 200 ms of round trip the guesses can't cover the trip and the delay and some stalls take over, as designed.
- **In a browser** (2026-11-09, `npm run build` and `wrangler dev`, two headless Chromium pages, `?netsim=90,25,6` on both): the guest's P1 moved a pixel from the console; the host saw the mismatch at the next hash, sent its state (16.6 KB in 2 parts), the guest loaded it and ran 34 frames back to the present in 3.7 ms, its readout showed RESYNC, and every hash after matched.
- **Testing it yourself.** `?netsim=LATENCY,JITTER,LOSS` on the URL delays everything this side sends by LATENCY ms (one way), give or take JITTER, and drops LOSS percent of game packets; put it on both pages (`?netsim=30,5,2` on each is about 60 ms of round trip). `net.setLocalSource(fn)`, `net.haltAt(frame)`, `net.stats()` (rollbacks, depths, costs, held and late events, confirmed frame) and `net.netcode.hashes` drive and check a scripted match.

## Performance

The target is a steady 60 fps (every frame under 16.7 ms, no long-frame spikes) on a mid-range phone about three years old (iPhone 12 or 13, Pixel 6a class), and playable on a $150 Android. **No real phone was measured for this pass.** Every number below comes from this repo's harness on an Apple M3 Pro, and each table says which stand-in it used. `?perf=1` (below) is how to check a real phone. School Chromebooks and software GL have their own section, with tier 0, the capability check and GRAPHICS (Chromebooks, below).

### The overlay: `?perf=1`

`play/?perf=1` (also forwarded from the homepage) shows a small panel over the game, refreshed four times a second: the frame interval (p50, p95 and p99 over the last 10 s, and fps), the main-thread work inside the frame and its split into sim, 2D draw, GL and HUD, long frames (over 25 ms) a minute, the last and the worst rollback re-sim (ms and frames), the tier (and whether it's adaptive or pinned), the device pixel ratio, the HUD canvas's scale and the GL canvas's size. It's `src/perf.js`: a few `performance.now()` reads a frame into fixed typed-array rings, always on (so `window.__perf.summary()` works on any page), with no garbage per frame. `?tier=0|1|2|3` pins a tier, as before, and `?cache=0` turns the scenery caches off for a comparison. The panel also shows the device's coarse buckets and the tier the capability check started at (Chromebooks, below), and "(N drawn)" when tier 0 is drawing every other frame.

### What changed, and why

- **The attract demo was choking on its own title.** The HUD drew every neon label with a live `shadowBlur`, and the attract card's 70 px "STICK FIGHT" with a 35-50 px blur that changes every frame made Chrome's canvas flush (`Canvas2DResourceProviderSharedImage::ProduceCanvasResource`) take ~3.1 s of every 4 s on the main thread: 10 fps at a 4x CPU throttle on the demo the owner saw at 20 fps on a Pixel 7. Neon text is now drawn once into a small bitmap at the HUD's resolution and stamped from then on (`neonText` in `src/combat/hud.js`: keyed by text, size, color, font, weight and glow in 0.05 steps, the 96 most recent kept, emptied when a web font finishes loading; text widths are cached the same way). Under any transform but the HUD's own, a label draws live as before.
- **Static scenery is cached** (`src/arena/cache.js`). Both floors of the building and the still part of its glow are drawn into world-space bitmaps covering the view plus a 50% margin, at the camera's zoom rounded up to the next 1/8 octave (never stretched, so they stay crisp at the close framing). They're redrawn only when the camera leaves the region, the zoom moves out of the bitmap's range (it draws a step ahead when zooming in, and serves until it's 30% bigger than needed), or the key changes (the mirror, the palette). The background goes straight to the compositor as a texture with a uv map (`fx.bgSource`, `u_bgMap` in the scene shader), so it's neither drawn nor uploaded per frame; a door swinging open (horde) draws it live for that second. The glow cache is one `drawImage` into the emissive layer, and what moves is drawn live over it (`drawBuildingEmissive(ctx, cam, lights, 'static' | 'live')`): the city's lit windows (now one prebuilt `Path2D` per tower instead of a hash and an occlusion test per window per frame), the flickering tubes, the vending machine and the fairy lights. A redraw is 0.3-0.5 ms of main thread at a 6x throttle (the first one, up to 37 ms); in a CPU fight it happens about every 1-2 s. The far skyline already had its own cache.
- **The scene shader walks the lights once.** The background light (with contact shadows), the figures' light and rim, and the haze were three loops over the same light array per pixel; they're one now (`lightAll` in `src/fx/shaders.js`), with the same sums, and the figure terms only where there's a figure.
- **The HUD canvas is no bigger than the screen it covers.** It was 2x the 1280x720 stage everywhere; a Pixel 7 held sideways shows the stage about 1920 device px wide, so it drew 2560 and shrank it. Now it's the stage's real width in device px (in 0.25 steps, 1x to 2x). The GL canvas stays 1280x720 (`createFX` sizes it), upscaled by the browser.
- **Less garbage.** Particle lighting (`fx.lightAt` per particle, over every light, with `Math.hypot`) and the particles' screen positions now write into scratch arrays: a 1v1 allocates 22 MB a second instead of 42 (Chrome's sampling heap profiler, `scripts/perf/profile.mjs --alloc`). What's left is spread thin: particle spawns, HUD color strings.
- **Rollback.** The reach table (~190 entries) was copied into every snapshot and rewritten by every active hit test; it now has a version that changes only when a value does, and snapshots of one version share one frozen copy (`touchReach` in `src/combat/snapshot.js`, the version kept off the snapshot in a WeakMap so a snapshot's content is still exactly the state). `saveState` 42 → 17 µs, `loadState` 43 → 18 µs. `segSeg`'s closure is hoisted, `penetration` walks index loops instead of destructuring iterators (100 pairs a step), and the CPU copies its opponent with `for...in` instead of `Object.assign`: a sim step 0.061 → 0.037 ms at the median (Node, M3, `scripts/perf/simbench.mjs`). Draw-only work (the life layer, cloth, `frameDraw`) runs from `render()` only, never from `sim.tick()`, so a re-sim never pays for it. The sim is unchanged: 48,000 steps of seeded CPU bouts hash the same as before, and `npm run netsim:proof` passes (0 of 5,587 restored steps and 0 of 4,320 resynced steps differ). No `NET_VERSION` bump.
- **Load.** The two faces are self-hosted (Latin subsets, 30 KB, `public/fonts/`, with their OFL files) and preloaded, instead of a render-blocking stylesheet from another origin, and the first frame no longer waits up to 2.5 s for them: the game draws at once and the HUD redraws in the real faces when they land. `__gauntlet.ready` still waits for the fonts, so captures see no load race. The bundle is all game code; the online code could be split out, but `main.js` uses it synchronously and that refactor wasn't worth the ~40 KB.
- **Tiers.** Each step down is a deliberate trade, not a blur (`TIERS` in `src/fx/index.js`): tier 2 drops the contact shadows and star streaks, keeps 20 lights and lights the scene at 0.8 of the canvas (64% of the pixels); tier 1 also drops the haze and the widest glow, keeps 12 lights, and draws the scene and the backdrop at half size but the fighters at 0.75, so their edges and outlines stay sharper than a uniform half-size tier. That costs about 10% more GPU work than the old all-half tier 1 (the SwiftShader table below), set in `TIERS[1].figScale`. Tier 2 used to be only 6% cheaper than tier 3; now it's about 25% cheaper. `scripts/perf/results/tiers.jpg` shows them side by side (tier 0 too, since 2026-11-11).
- **The adaptive tier.** Where it starts is now the capability check's call (Chromebooks, below); before it, phones started at tier 2 and everything else at 3. Frame intervals are judged every half second; a window is slow only when both its average and its median miss ~55 fps, so one long frame (a GC, a cache redraw) never counts, and under ~33 fps it's very slow. One very slow window or two slow ones step down. A tier that failed becomes the ceiling (so it never flaps back and forth); a tier under the ceiling is climbed back to after 5 good seconds, and a failed tier gets one retry after a good minute. The window after a change is skipped while the canvases resize. Tested with a scripted per-frame load: 0 ms then 22 ms then a single 300 ms hitch then 9 ms. It stepped down from tier 3 to 2 within a second of the load and to 1 a second and a half later, the hitch changed nothing, and it never flapped.
- **What players get.** The `perf` analytics event (see Analytics) sends, once a session, the tier it settled on, where it started, touch or mouse, the frame time p95 and p50, and since 2026-11-11 the device's coarse buckets, what held the frame back and the GRAPHICS choice (Chromebooks, below; the sample query is in Analytics). For example: `SELECT blob2 AS tier, blob4 AS input, count() AS n, avg(double1) AS p50 FROM stick_fight_events WHERE blob1 = 'perf' GROUP BY tier, input`.

### Measured

`npm run build`, then `node scripts/perf/run.mjs` serves `dist/` (gzipped, as Cloudflare compresses it) and plays each scenario in headless Chromium on the Mac's real GPU (ANGLE Metal), emulating a Pixel 7 held sideways (915x412 CSS px at DPR 2.625, touch), with the CPU throttled through DevTools (1x, 4x and 6x; 4x is the usual stand-in for a mid-range phone, 6x for a cheap one), after a 3 s warm-up (12 s for horde, so the pile builds). "Before" is `main` at 88bbc8a with only the profiler added. The scenarios: **close**, a CPU 1v1 (THE MANAGER v WELLNESS COACH, cloth on both) that spends most of its time in the close framing; **horde**, `horde-ai` with two goons and a growing pile; **rain**, CHEF v RECEPTIONIST under the default night-rain look (every stage rains through the smashed pane); **floor**, `floor-break-test`; **attract**, the demo. Headless Chromium runs rAF at 120 Hz, so an unthrottled frame is 8.3 ms; a phone runs 60 to 120.

Read the work columns as the CPU cost (time inside the frame on the main thread), and the frame columns as the cadence that results. Neither shows the phone's GPU: the M3's GPU is several times faster than a phone's, so these runs aren't GPU-bound.

**The rollback burst** is the worst correction: load a snapshot, then 12 steps (`MAX_ROLLBACK`), each saving a snapshot first as the netcode does, on a live fight in the page (`run.mjs --rollback`, 40 bursts). At 6x the first after-run's worst was one 68.7 ms burst; two reruns gave 7.8 / 6.1 ms (close) and 8.0 / 9.5 ms (horde) worst, and 0.5-5.7 ms at the median. The in-page timings at 6x swing a lot from run to run.

| Rollback burst (12 steps) | CPU | p50 ms before → after | worst ms before → after |
|---|---|---|---|
| close | 1x | 2.2 → 0.5 | 5.8 → 0.9 |
| close | 4x | 6.2 → 3.0 | 21.0 → 7.9 |
| close | 6x | 12.1 → 8.0 | 31.1 → 68.7 |
| horde | 1x | 3.1 → 1.0 | 6.1 → 1.8 |
| horde | 4x | 5.4 → 3.9 | 17.4 → 7.0 |
| horde | 6x | 7.1 → 5.2 | 15.9 → 10.6 |

**Load** is a cold `/play/` (cache off, three runs averaged; `run.mjs --load`, `--net` for a phone's 4G: 150 ms round trip, 9 Mbps). "First frame" is the game's first drawn frame (`performance.mark('sf:first-frame')`; before this pass it was the moment fonts arrived and the first render ran). First paint got a little later, the main thread is busier during load because the game now renders while the fonts arrive instead of waiting on them, and the 282 KB includes the fonts (the Google-hosted ones weren't counted before, being cross-origin).

| Load `/play/` | CPU | Network | First frame ms | First paint ms | Main thread ms | KB |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| cold | 1x | local | 409 → 206 | 251 → 160 | 130 → 160 | 249 → 282 |
| cold | 4x | local | 777 → 485 | 487 → 584 | 460 → 611 | 249 → 282 |
| cold | 4x | 4g | 1534 → 1139 | 1041 → 1248 | 453 → 632 | 249 → 282 |

**GPU work** can't be seen on the M3 (its GPU finishes in ~3 ms, under the sync's own overhead), so it's measured with SwiftShader, Chrome's software GL, whose time grows with the shader work per pixel: relative, between builds and tiers, as a stand-in for a phone GPU's fill cost (`run.mjs --gpu --angle swiftshader`: each frame is stepped, drawn, and followed by a 1-pixel `readPixels` that waits for it).

| GPU work (SwiftShader, ms a frame) | Tier | p50 before → after | p95 before → after |

| close | 3 | 305 → 234 | 426 → 280 |
| close | 2 | 286 → 179 | 319 → 211 |
| close | 1 | 112 → 113 | 126 → 140 |
| horde | 3 | 346 → 300 | 382 → 394 |
| horde | 2 | 320 → 234 | 355 → 335 |
| horde | 1 | 128 → 138 | 144 → 190 |
| attract | 3 | 302 → 248 | 324 → 275 |
| attract | 2 | 275 → 188 | 302 → 220 |
| attract | 1 | 105 → 127 | 119 → 175 |

**The adaptive tier** on the same Mac, from the phone's starting tier 2 (`--tier auto`). The M3's GPU has the headroom, so it climbs to 3; the step-down was tested with the scripted load above.

| Adaptive tier (phone start: 2) | CPU | settled on | fps | p95 ms |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| attract | 4x | tier 3 | 119 | 9.2 |
| attract | 6x | tier 3 | 116 | 9.2 |
| close | 4x | tier 3 | 115 | 9.3 |
| close | 6x | tier 3 | 109 | 16.7 |
| horde | 4x | tier 3 | 119 | 9.1 |
| horde | 6x | tier 3 | 113 | 16.5 |

**The look at tier 3** is unchanged: `node scripts/perf/compare.mjs <main's dist> <out>` steps the same seeded scenes frame by frame in both builds, with `Math.random`, the particles and the arena clock seeded, and compares the screenshots. RMSE against main is 0.004 to 0.024 (main against itself: 0.0005 to 0.0075). The differences are sub-pixel: the cached background is sampled rather than drawn at the exact zoom, the city windows are antialiased as one path, and HUD text is stamped on whole device pixels. `scripts/perf/results/tier3-before-after.jpg` shows three scenes side by side. Fighters don't glow.

**Not done, and worth doing with a real phone in hand:** a GPU trace on an actual Mali or Apple GPU (the SwiftShader numbers are relative only), the HUD's other `shadowBlur`s (the bars and the timer ring, which change size every frame), and pooling particle objects.

### Chromebooks (2026-11-11)

Reports came in that the game runs **super slow on Chromebooks**, which is a lot of the middle-school players: school Chromebooks have Celeron or MediaTek chips, Intel UHD 600 or Mali GPUs, 4 GB of RAM, and some run WebGL on software. Tier 1 was the floor before this, every tier was the full WebGL lighting, and a desktop-class pointer (a Chromebook's trackpad) started at tier 3 and found out the slow way. Now the game guesses the device at boot, has a tier 0 built for a weak machine, and remembers where it settled.

**No real Chromebook was measured.** The stand-in is this repo's harness on the M3 Pro: Chromium on **SwiftShader** (software GL, the worst case), the CPU throttled **6x** through DevTools (an M3 core is about 7x a Celeron N4020's single-thread score), at a Chromebook's 1366x768 at DPR 1 and 1920x1080 at DPR 1.25 (`run.mjs --device cros|croshd --angle swiftshader`). The machine was shared with other agents' test runs (load average 6 to 30), so read single numbers as plus or minus 15%. To try it on a real one: `neonstickfight.com/play/?perf=1` shows the frame time, the tier, the device buckets and the tier the check started at; `?tier=0` pins tier 0; SETTINGS › GRAPHICS picks a tier for good.

**Tier 0** (`TIERS[0]` in `src/fx/index.js`, `FLAT_FS` in `src/fx/shaders.js`):
- **GPU:** one flat pass straight to a canvas half the size each way (640x360, the browser scales it up), every layer at half size, the fighters too. No per-pixel lights: the room is lit by one color for the frame, most of the ambient plus a little of the average color of the lights on screen, so a red-lit stage still reads red. No bloom chain, no star streaks, no reflections, no protect mask, no final pass. What stays: the sky, the skyline, the fog band, the neon faces with a short glow from eight taps of the emissive layer, the dark edge around every fighter and body, the foreground, the hit flash, and the grade's cheap part (contrast, saturation, the toe). Fighters are drawn in their own flat color and the glow is masked off them: fighters never glow, and the HUD is unchanged (its text doesn't glow). `scripts/perf/results/tiers.jpg` (a CPU 1v1) and `tiers-horde.jpg` have tiers 3, 2, 1 and 0 side by side (`tiers.mjs`).
- **CPU:** no drifting motes, half the rain, a third of the debris a hit throws, the cloth hanging at rest instead of swinging (`life.cloth`), and the city's lit windows from one bitmap (below).
- **The sim and the netcode don't change.** Only drawing does: every knob is in `render()` or the compositor, never in `sim.tick()`. `npm run netsim:proof` passes (below).
- **The last resort: every other frame.** At tier 0 under ~20 fps for a second, the picture is drawn every other refresh while the sim steps every refresh (`fx.drawEvery`, `frame()` in `src/main.js`; render()'s own clocks get the time since the last drawn frame). It's that low because the sim already keeps time down to 10 fps (up to 60 steps a frame, a frame counted as at most 100 ms), so above 20 fps skipping pictures only loses pictures: horde on the stand-in drew 31-35 a second every frame and 28 every other. It tries every frame again after 5 s, then 10, 20 and so on, each fall back doubling the wait. **On the stand-in it never beat drawing every frame**: rAF is paced by the GPU and vsync, so skipping a picture barely shortens the refresh (close at 1x on SwiftShader: 41 drawn a second every frame, 18 every other). It stays as a guarded last resort for a machine under 20 fps, with the timed retry so it can't trap a machine that would do better drawing every frame; real `?perf=1` numbers (the overlay says "(N drawn)") should decide whether to keep it.

**What was really slow, found by profiling, not guessing** (`trace.mjs --all`, `run.mjs --gpu --off ...`):
- **Software GL turned every 2D canvas into a GPU job.** On SwiftShader, Chrome rasterized the game's 2D canvases (the six layers, the HUD, the scenery caches) through the software GL: 1.8 s of every 3 s on the GPU thread (`DoEndRasterCHROMIUM`), against 0.5 s for the WebGL. `src/softcanvas.js`, imported first, reads the GL renderer from a throwaway 1x1 context (5 to 18 ms here, on a busy machine) and, on a software GL only, makes every 2D context ask for `willReadFrequently`, which keeps it on the CPU. It has to be all of them: copying an accelerated canvas into a CPU one (the HUD's text stamps, the scenery caches into the layers) reads it back every time, and switching only the layers made it slower. Tier 0 went from 16 fps to 44 on this one change. (A real Chromebook whose GPU is blocklisted already rasters on the CPU; the harness's `--use-angle=swiftshader` is what made the canvases go through it, so this is the honest worst case either way.)
- **The city's lit windows** were ~30 ms of a 51 ms tier 0 frame on the GPU thread: a few thousand rects in a `Path2D` per tower, filled under two clips every frame. The far layer's mapping cancels the camera's zoom, so the city is always drawn at the layer's own scale; at half-size layers (tiers 1 and 0) it now comes from one bitmap per scale, placed on whole device pixels so the windows stay sharp (`cityBitmap` in `src/arena/building.js`). Full-size layers (tiers 3 and 2) draw the paths as before, so their look is unchanged.
- **Not the problem:** the HUD (0.2-0.3 ms of main thread a frame; hiding it changed nothing), the body pile in horde (skipping it changed nothing), the scenery cache's blit, and premultiplied or unflipped uploads (2 ms of main thread at 6x, no change in the frame rate, so not done). The tier 0 flat pass itself is 5.5 ms on SwiftShader.

**The capability check** (`src/device.js`, called from `src/main.js` right after the compositor is made; it reads values the browser already has, well under 50 ms, no test scene). The GL renderer string is sorted into a family and never kept: `software` (SwiftShader, llvmpipe, Microsoft Basic Render), `intel`, `mali`, `adreno`, `powervr`, `apple`, `nvidia-amd`, `other`. The OS comes from the user agent (`cros`, `android`, `ios`, `mac`, `win`, `linux`, `other`), the cores from `navigator.hardwareConcurrency` (`le2`, `4`, `6plus`), the memory from `navigator.deviceMemory` (`le4`, `8plus`, `unknown`; Chrome only). The start tier:
- software GL → **tier 0**;
- a Chromebook, or 4 cores or fewer, or 4 GB or less → **tier 1** (on the stand-in tier 1 is 10-11 fps on SwiftShader; a real Chromebook with its GPU should do much better, and the adaptive tier moves it either way);
- a phone → tier 2; anything else → tier 3.

The adaptive tier then works as before, now down to 0: two slow half-seconds or one very slow one step down a tier (from 1 to 0 like any other step: the frame interval counts whatever made it long, main thread or GPU, and tier 0 cuts both), and 5 good seconds climb one. The attract demo behind the title is the warm-up: it runs the full scene, so by the time a player has picked a fighter the tier has had the seconds it needs, and the first fight doesn't start slow. `npm run test:device` drives a scripted load through it: 3 → 2 → 1 → 0 under 22 ms of extra work a frame, every other frame under 60 ms, and every frame again once the load stops (the tiers that failed stay the ceiling until a good minute earns one retry, as before).

**The remembered tier** (`src/gfxPrefs.js`, localStorage `stick-fight-gfx`, wrapped in try/catch). Once AUTO has held one tier for 15 s, it's saved; the next visit on this device starts there instead of at the check's guess, with every tier still open to climb to.

**GRAPHICS in SETTINGS:** AUTO, HIGH, MEDIUM, LOW, LOWEST (tiers 3 to 0). AUTO, the default, is the adaptive tier, and the note beside the label says what it's drawing at now (`AUTO · NOW LOW`), the way CONTROLS says SCREEN FITS; a fixed choice says FIXED and turns adaptation off. Keys and the pad step it with left and right. It's saved on the device like the touch size but, unlike the touch size, not carried to the account: a Chromebook and a desktop want different answers. `?tier=` still beats both.

**Measured.** "Before" is `main` at c75cae0 with the harness changes only; it had no tier 0, and its AUTO on a Chromebook started at 3 and could only reach tier 1, so the AUTO rows' before is the before build's tier 1 (its best case). "fps" is pictures drawn a second. "<2" is a run where every frame took over half a second, which the profiler drops as a pause. The before runs swing a lot (the rain row at tier 3 caught a lucky stretch). The AUTO rows were measured again on their own after the last change to the every-other-frame rule (`cros-auto.json`, `croshd-auto.json`), and the throttle table's attract row at 1x caught a load spike (the machine's load went from 7 to 30 during it).
